So the renderer keeps an anchor floor, set at the reader's latest anchor (a prime, a resync or a correction; for an anchor before the floor formed, the floor once formed) to what that callback's Jt counted from: the floor, or, while the floor lay below the anchor floor, the lower of the anchor floor and the higher of the callback's value and the one of the timed callback before it, never below the floor. It gives the reader Jf, how far the floor lies above the anchor floor, in capture frames, rounded toward zero. While the floor lies below the anchor floor, Jt counts from the anchor floor and Jf is how far the higher of the callback's value and the floor lies below the anchor floor, 0 when the callback's value is not below it; `Jt + Jf` is the same either way, so the drift is judged the same, but an anchor lands by the smaller Jt. The anchor floor never rises until the next anchor; it falls toward each callback whose value lies below it, by the share `1 − e^(−t / 2 s)` of the gap, t being that callback's device frames in seconds, and each fall lands no lower than that callback's value. Every read judges its error `e = (W − P) − (De + Jf)` against the dead band and still anchors at `W − De`, so the anchor lands as S209 says and Jf is 0 again from the next callback. S207's tracking takes the fill at `now − Jt − Jf`. Without both times Jf is 0. In the core's 600-second runs with 512-frame blocks at 48 kHz, a 44.1 kHz output whose callbacks come up to 40 ms later along a triangle over 100 s (0.8 ms a second) and, about 1.5 times a second, 5 to 15.4 ms later still, on a clock in step: held back 100 ms, from 2 s on no read runs dry and no drift correction is made (before, 6 drift skips and 5 drift silences), and at hold-back 0 or held back, every delay lies within 2 ms (before, 40 ms apart at hold-back 0). On a clock 300 ppm fast or slow over 300 s under the same callbacks, the output at hold-back 0 and, since S207 trims at any hold-back, the one held back are each trimmed one way by at least nine tenths of what the clock gained and at most 16 frames the other way, with no correction from 2 s on (before, the one at hold-back 0 both ways by thousands of frames, and the one held back corrected at T).
  - *Why the anchor floor falls* (the core's timing matrix, design step 5; core tests "timing matrix: ...", "render: the anchor floor falls ..."). A device whose callbacks come earlier than any its anchor knew of shows that the anchor lay too close to W, by as much: an output that anchored during a late stretch of its callbacks played that much early all session, two Bluetooth outputs whose lateness wandered at different phases stayed some 21 ms apart for a whole run, and an output at the capture rate that anchored so ran dry. With the anchor floor lower, the target moves later by as much and S207's trim carries the output there, a drop past T being stepped back by the dead band. It falls over seconds, not at once, because two stray early stamps would otherwise move an output by their whole size, up to 50 ms, until the next anchor: in the matrix, following the floor at once moved 14 of the 15 outputs with two stray stamps of 21 to 80 ms by more than 2 ms, up to 57 ms, and failed 7 of its 48 cases; with the fall over 2 s, every case passes and none of those 48 moved an output by more than 0.8 ms; longer callbacks can move one further, 1.24 ms in the case below. On a slow clock the trim that carries an output to the lower anchor floor runs against the clock, so the matrix's trim invariant allows that fall on top of its 16 frames: a 44.1 kHz output with 1024-frame callbacks, whose two strays 54 ms early lower the anchor floor 1.24 ms (60 frames), is slowed by 72 frames against its clock 68 ppm slow ("timing matrix: late-first cases on draws of their own ..."). A follower that also rose, slowly (a 32 s time constant on at most 2.5 ms of the gap), failed the steadiness invariant in 22 cases, because it followed the callbacks' wander up. Two stray stamps also leave the floor below the anchor floor for up to 50 s (core test "render: a resync after two stray early stamps ..."): Jt counted from that floor made an output on time count every callback as late, and a resync in that time landed the strays' size later until the next anchor, 19.9 ms for strays 20 ms early and 49.9 ms for 60 ms; counted from the anchor floor, it lands within 2 ms of where it would without them and counts no late callback. A third stray on the anchoring callback itself used to set the anchor floor to its value at once (core test "render: a resync on a third stray early stamp ..."): the output landed right, but every later callback counted the strays' size, up to 50 ms, as late until the floor leaked back up, and its drift was judged that much off; taking the higher of that value and the previous callback's, the anchor floor stays put and all of it holds within 2 ms with no late callback. Two strays in a row on the anchor and the callback before it still set it. An anchor during the anchor floor's fall after a late start lands on its delay too ("render: a resync while the anchor floor falls ..."). Strays among the callbacks the floor forms over are not covered: they form the floor and the anchor floor (the first two callbacks 80 ms early, below).
  - *A late start* (core test "timing matrix: a device whose first callbacks are late ..."). An output whose first callbacks come late primes and forms its floor on them, so once they come on time the floor drops by their lateness and the anchor floor falls after it, moving the output later by as much. Silence from a dry read, and the part of an S206 slow-down kept for a shortage, already move the output later: each pays toward what the anchor floor still has to fall, down to the floor or that callback's value when lower, and the trim's reference, while it is being measured, is rebased by what is paid, so the output does not move by it twice. A dry read whose silence is paid in full keeps the trim's reference, so the trim goes on following the rest of the fall; restarting it there lost what the anchor floor fell before the new reference was taken. The trim carries the output at most a 256th of each callback's reads, at least a frame (S207), so 45 ms take about 12 s at 512-frame callbacks and 22 s at 480-frame ones at 48 kHz (one frame per 10 ms), and the output plays that much early against the others meanwhile. Inside a renderer's start window (S317) the fall is therefore made in one step (S318). The carry above, and the matrix's bound for it (I6 judges a late-first output's trim from 20 s after its callbacks came on time, or from when its trim can have carried it their lateness plus 2 s, whichever is later), hold only for a fall the window does not catch (S319); a late start the window catches is judged from the window's end plus 2 s (S320).
  - *Without both times* (the Windows renderer, which gets no output time and whose late wakes ask for more frames instead, S206), Jr is 0 and nothing changes. A callback on time has Jr = 0, so steady callbacks play the very same frames with output times as without.
  - *Counters.* The renderer counts the largest Jt and the callbacks with Jt above 5 ms; the Mac logs both when an output stops, next to the reader's counters, so a session shows whether its callbacks came late.
  - In the core's 60-second runs with 512-frame blocks at 48 kHz, a 44.1 kHz output at hold-back 0 and another 20 ms later, a 48 kHz output 43 ms later, all three with callbacks up to 30 ms late, and a 48 kHz output on time 203 ms later, every hold-back set again every 3 s: from 2 s on no read runs dry and no drift correction is made, and each output's delay stays within a block and 2 ms. Without S209 the output at hold-back 0 ran dry 18 times and its delay spread over about 34 ms. With each output's first callback 80 ms early, nothing changes either, and outputs on time play the very same frames as without that callback. With the first two callbacks 80 ms early, both set the floor: each output's delay moves by at most 50 ms past its delay without them, no read runs dry and no drift correction is made from 2 s on, and from 50 s on every delay lies within a block and 2 ms of the delays without them.
- **S317** *Required* (AudioSync design; the core's timing matrix: a 48 kHz output with 480-frame callbacks whose first callbacks came 45 ms late played up to 45 ms early against the other outputs for about 22 s, S209 "A late start", core test "timing matrix: late-first cases on draws of their own ..."; heard as outputs out of sync at the start). The start window. A renderer may correct the error a late start leaves in one step only inside a short window after it starts, while its device's first callbacks still settle and before the output has played long enough for a step to break continuous sound.
  - *What opens it.* The window opens with the renderer's first timed callback (one with both the time it reads at and its first frame's output time, S209), once per renderer: an output switched on, every output at a session start, and an output the host makes anew after its device restarted or changed its format. Those are the callbacks the renderer forms its lateness floor on (S209), and a device's first callbacks are the ones that may come late. A resync, a delay or hold-back change (S187, S208), a drift correction (S188), a capture stall (S53) or a source change keeps the renderer, its floor and its anchor floor, so it opens no window: the output plays on, and S209 already lands such an anchor on its delay.
  - *How long.* The window closes 3 s of device frames after its first timed callback, or when the output's stop fade-out is asked for (S185), whichever comes first. It has to hold the longest late stretch the core's timing matrix gives a late start, 1.8 s (`core/tests/timing_matrix.rs:40`), then the run that confirms the callbacks came on time (S318), which takes as long as the floor takes to form, at least 5 callbacks and 300 ms (`FLOOR_FORM_CALLBACKS` and `FLOOR_FORM_MS`, `core/src/render.rs:180-184`), 464 ms with the largest callbacks the core runs, 4096 frames at 44.1 kHz (S207), then the step's fade-out of 20 ms (S182, `core/src/fade.rs:29`): 2.3 s, rounded up to whole seconds. It stays that short because after it the output has played seconds of continuous sound, which a step would break (S319).
  - *Without both times* (the Windows renderer, S209) there is no floor, so no window, and nothing changes.
- **S318** *Required* (S317's sources). The start step. Inside its window, a renderer whose device's callbacks turn out to come earlier than its anchor knew corrects the whole remaining error in one step instead of S209's fall and S207's trim of at most a 256th of each callback's reads.
  - *The run.* Once its reader has anchored, the renderer takes each timed callback's value as an anchor does (S209: the higher of its `now − output time` and the previous timed callback's). A callback whose value lies more than 2 ms below the anchor floor extends a run; any other ends it. A run of at least 5 callbacks and 300 ms of device frames, the evidence the floor forms on (S209), confirms the move: one or two stray early stamps never make one, so S209's protection against them holds. Why 2 ms: a smaller gap lies within the matrix's tolerance for an output's delay (I4 and I5, 2 ms, `core/tests/timing_matrix.rs:91-92`), S209's fall and S207's trim carry it within about a second even at one frame per 480-frame callback, and it lies well above S207's dead zone (15 frames, 0.3 ms) and the 0.8 ms two stray stamps moved an output in the matrix (S209).
  - *The step.* When a run is confirmed, the renderer sets the anchor floor to the highest value in the run, never below the floor, so the output moves later by the least the run showed, and its reader anchors anew at `W − De` against it, as S46's resync does. The output then plays where an anchor on an on-time callback would have put it (S209), the whole remaining error at once. Silence from dry reads and the kept part of S206's slow-downs, which already paid toward the anchor floor's fall (S209), are part of where the reader stands, so the anchor counts them once. The tracking restarts and measures a new reference (S207). The step only moves an output later, since the anchor floor never rises until the next anchor (S209): the reader inserts the step as silence (S47), plays on contiguously from the frame after the last one it emitted, and skips no audio. After a step a new run is taken against the new anchor floor, so a device whose callbacks move earlier twice inside the window gets two steps.
  - *Making it unheard, or acceptable.* While nothing is audible (the reader has not primed, or its frames so far are silence, or the stream is closed, S184), the step lands at once, without a fade. While the output plays, the step is made as a held correction (S188): the renderer fades out over F (20 ms, S182), sets the new anchor floor after the fade-out's last frame, and its next read anchors; the audio after it fades in (S184). The listener hears, once and within the output's first 3 s, a 20 ms fade-out, the step's silence (at most 50 ms, S209's cap on lateness) and a 20 ms fade-in: a short dip while the output is still starting, against up to 22 s of one output playing up to 45 ms ahead of the others. A delay change or held correction already waiting (S187, S188) carries the step: its anchor uses the new anchor floor, so one fade covers both. No step is made once a stop's fade-out was asked for.
  - *Per output.* Each renderer decides from its own callbacks only, and a step moves only its own output. It changes neither the renderer's start lead nor its hold-back, so the session lead K (S208) and every other output, local or AirPlay, stay where they are, and nothing re-times (S177).
  - *Counting.* A step is neither a drift correction (S50 to S53) nor a dry read, and the reader's counters of those don't count it. The renderer counts its start steps and the capture frames they moved its output, apart from S207's trims and S206's slow-downs.
- **S319** *Required* (S317's sources; S207, S209). After the window. A device whose callbacks come earlier only after its renderer's window closed, or a run still unconfirmed when it closes, is followed as S209 says and nothing more: the anchor floor falls toward the callbacks over `ANCHOR_FALL_MS`, dry reads and kept slow-downs pay toward the fall, and S207's trim carries the output at most a 256th of each callback's reads and at least a frame. No step is made then, however large the gap: the output plays continuous sound by then, and a step would be heard as a dropout in it. A run unconfirmed at the window's end is dropped, and what the anchor floor already fell stays. The matrix's I6 bound for such a fall is unchanged (S209 "A late start").
- **S320** *Required* (S317's sources; the core test to come, in `core/tests/timing_matrix.rs`). What the timing matrix shows.
  - *A late start the window catches.* In each of the matrix's late-first cases (first callbacks 15.4 or 45 ms late for 0.6 to 1.8 s, all inside the window), the late output makes exactly one start step, inside its window, and no drift correction (I2 counts none, the step included). From the window's end its delay lies within 2 ms of its on-time twin's, and I6 judges its trim from the window's end plus one `ANCHOR_FALL_MS`, with the same 16 frames against its clock as an output on time and no extension for the trim's cap. I1, I3 to I5 and I7 hold as before.
  - *No step mid-stream.* No output on time makes a step, and no output makes one after its window: in the 48 cases, the 16 stall cases (I8 included) and the late-first cases' other outputs, every renderer counts zero start steps.
  - *The red test.* The matrix's case f20 (the 480-frame output at 48 kHz whose first callbacks come 45 ms late; core test "timing matrix: late-first cases on draws of their own ...") has its delay within 2 ms of its on-time twin's from 3 s after its first callback on, with one start step and no drift correction. Without S318 the test fails: the trim carries the output at most 2.1 ms a second, so it reaches its delay only about 22 s after its callbacks came on time (S209 "A late start").
- **S229** *Required* (the Mac's sitting of 2026-10-21: two or more Bluetooth outputs chopped by ear while every counter the app kept stayed clean; core tests "observer: ..."). The observer. Per local output the renderer keeps a few values that say how close its reader came to running dry and whether the device's timeline moved, for the debug log. They only measure: nothing that plays changes with them.
  - *Warm-up.* The renderer counts the device frames of the callbacks that begin while it plays (not closed, S184). Once they reach 500 ms, the span over which S207's trim measures its reference, every later callback is observed; before that nothing is kept.
  - *Least slack.* At the start of every observed callback that begins while the output plays and before a stop's fade-out was asked for, before any trim, the renderer takes `W − P` where its reader stands, less the capture frames the callback needs (S62), in ns at the capture rate, and keeps the least. A callback whose reader would anchor instead is skipped. Below 0, a callback found fewer frames than it needed: S206's slow-down made up the shortage, or the read ran dry. Until the first observed value there is none.
  - *Floor drop.* With output times (S209), the most the lateness floor fell below the anchor floor set at the reader's latest anchor, in ns: how far the anchor floor can fall toward a drop that lasts. 0 without output times.
  - *Output-time steps.* With output times, the observed callbacks whose output time lies more than 1 ms off the previous callback's output time plus that callback's frames at the device's rate. 0 without.
  - *Shortages.* From the start, like the reader's counters, the renderer counts the capture frames by which S206's slow-down made up shortages, apart from the trims that follow the capture's clock (S207).
  - *Hosts.* Each value is an atomic the control side reads from any thread (S190); a callback publishes only a new extreme. The Mac also counts each output device's IO overloads (the HAL's processor overload notifications) and logs, per output, these values, the Bluetooth outputs playing and the reader's counters one minute after the output starts and when it stops, and once per session the Wi-Fi band, channel and signal strength where macOS gives them without a permission prompt. The Windows app adds the least slack and the shortages to its status line.
  - In the core's runs with 512-frame blocks at 48 kHz and 512-frame callbacks: a 44.1 kHz output at hold-back 0 on the capture's clock keeps a least slack of about 20 ms and counts no shortage, floor drop or step, while a 48 kHz output, whose lead is one callback, reads the newest block whole, a least slack of exactly 0. On a clock 2000 ppm fast without stamps, the 44.1 kHz output's least slack falls below 0 and its shortages are counted. Callbacks 10 ms earlier than at the anchor show a floor drop of 10 ms; output times that jump 5 ms count one step and a floor drop of 5 ms, and output times 0.4 ms off either way count none.
- **S230** *Required* (the observer's runs, S229: a 48 kHz output whose lead is one callback keeps a least slack of exactly 0, so a callback that comes a block early runs dry; a backstop until the observer's values from hardware show how much slack Bluetooth outputs need; core tests "render start: a Bluetooth output ...", "timing matrix: a Bluetooth output ..."). Bluetooth outputs. The host tells each renderer, before its first callback, whether its device is Bluetooth: on the Mac a device whose transport is Bluetooth or Bluetooth LE; on Windows an endpoint whose device enumerator is a Bluetooth one (`BTHENUM`, `BTHLEDevice` and the other names starting with `BTH`), the test by which the app shows it as a Bluetooth speaker. A Bluetooth output's start lead ℓ (S208) counts one capture block more: 512 capture frames, the block the Mac's capture writes per IO cycle by default (a Windows packet holds 480), about 10.7 ms at 48 kHz. Nothing else changes: S206's floor Lr, S207's trim and the observer stay as they are, and every other output publishes the same ℓ as before.
  - *What moves.* The block enters K like any start lead, so the outputs keep exactly the offsets the user set (S208). It moves an output only when the Bluetooth output sets K: then every local output plays later against the capture, and so against the screen, by as much as K grows, a block at most (by default about 10.7 ms). With an AirPlay speaker chosen K is 0 and nothing moves, and a Bluetooth output held back by at least its new ℓ less K moves nothing.
  - *Why a block.* Bluetooth sinks get their callbacks at irregular times (S209). An output whose reader anchored in a callback just after a capture write and whose later callbacks come just before one finds a block fewer frames than at its anchor; with a lead of one callback it runs dry. The block covers that phase, not a device clock that runs fast, which S206 and S207 follow.
  - *Log.* The line each host logs when an output starts names a Bluetooth output's extra lead in ms.
  - In the core's 60-second runs with 512-frame blocks at 48 kHz, the capture's first block after the start on time and every later one up to 1 ms after the callback that reads it, a 48 kHz output at hold-back 0 that sets the session lead next to a 44.1 kHz output held back 100 ms and a 96 kHz one held back 30 ms ran dry at its start, on the Mac's timed callbacks and on Windows, which left it up to a block behind the others. As Bluetooth it no longer runs dry, the three play within 0.1 ms of their offsets, the other two exactly a block later than before, and with 10 ms Windows periods its least slack is about 9.3 ms instead of 0.
- **S231** *Required* (Android's Bluetooth audio believes a device's delay report only between 50 and 1000 ms and falls back to a fixed estimate outside it (Android Open Source Project, Bluetooth audio HAL); a Bluetooth device may report no latency or a wrong one; core tests "render start: a Bluetooth output's implausible reported latency ..."). Reported latency of a Bluetooth output. At each start the Mac reads what comes after a frame's output time before it is heard: the device's and its output stream's latency plus the output unit's own (S185). For a Bluetooth output (S230) the renderer believes that report only from 50 to 1000 ms, both included. Outside that window it uses the latest report the window accepted for the same device, kept across its outputs and reconnects while the app runs, else 150 ms: the 149.3 ms a 44.1 kHz Bluetooth speaker reported on the Mac on 2026-10-20, rounded. Every other output's report is used as it is.
  - *What it feeds.* S185's wait for a stop's fade-out and the debug log, nothing else. The start lead ℓ and the session lead K (S208) never take a reported latency: they come from the callbacks, and what a device adds after its output time is the user's to set by offset (S176). A report of 0 ms or 30 s therefore moves no output; it only changes how long a stop waits, which S186 caps at 500 ms.
  - *Log.* A rejected report shows in the line the Mac logs when the output starts and in its observer line (S229): the value reported and the one used instead.
  - *Windows.* Nothing to window. The only latency WASAPI reports, `GetStreamLatency`, is the stream's inside the audio engine, not what a Bluetooth sink adds after the link; it read 0 on every endpoint measured on a PC, and S185's drain uses it only when the endpoint's clock gives no position. No reported latency enters K there either.

### Fades at stream boundaries

S182 to S191 take the click out of starts, stops, source changes and re-timings. A short click is heard on every source change and every start and stop on the Mac's local outputs, Bluetooth included: an output plays the first frame its reader copies at full level, whatever the source plays at that instant (S45); a stop halts every output in mid-waveform (S115); a source change does both (S141); and a delay change joins two unrelated points of the waveform or cuts to silence (S47, S48). Each of these boundaries gets a short fade. These clauses are design requirements: the Rust core, the Windows app and the Mac app follow them. The Windows app used to fade an output out over whatever one device callback held, which could be a few frames; S185 replaced that with a fixed length.

- **S182** *Required.* Fade shape and length. A fade is a gain w applied alike to every channel of a frame, over F = round(0.020 × R) frames at the rate R of the stream it is applied to (960 at 48 kHz, 882 at 44.1 kHz). For frame i = 0 … F − 1 of a fade, with x = (i + 1) / F: a fade-in has w = ½ − ½·cos(π·x), and w = 1 exactly at i = F − 1; a fade-out has w = ½ + ½·cos(π·x), and w = 0 exactly at i = F − 1. A stream is *open* (w = 1), *fading in*, *fading out* or *closed* (w = 0: exact zeros, whatever the input, NaN and ±infinity included). A fade-out requested while fading in, or a fade-in requested while fading out, continues from the frame of the other curve whose w is closest to the current one, so w never jumps; a request for the state a stream is already in or heading to changes nothing. Reasons: 20 ms is long enough that the ramp itself carries next to no energy above about 50 Hz (1 / 20 ms), so it isn't heard as a click even on bass-heavy material, and short enough to sound like a clean edge rather than a fade, while a stop waits only about that long (S186). A raised cosine has zero slope at both ends, so it has no corner where it meets silence or full level; its largest step between frames is π / (2F) of the signal (0.16 % at 48 kHz, against 0.10 % for a linear ramp of the same length, which has corners at both ends); and starting it a few frames early is inaudible: 16 frames early, the audio enters at 0.08 % (−62 dB) where a linear ramp would already be at 1.8 % (−35 dB), which S184 relies on.
- **S183** *Required.* Where a local output fades. Each local output's renderer applies its fade at the device rate, after rate conversion and channel mapping (S62, S63), as a factor of its own after the volume gain (S64, S71): the device gets the sample × the gain × w, so the last frame of a fade-out and every frame after it are exact zeros whatever the gain or the converter left. F counts device frames (S182 with R the device's rate). The fade never changes the gain or a stored volume; a gain change during a fade changes only the gain factor, with its own ramp (S64) as before, and an output at gain 0 stays exact zeros. NaN and ±infinity are replaced by 0 before the fade multiplies (S65), so w = 0 never turns them into NaN.
- **S184** *Required.* Fade-in when audio starts. A renderer starts closed. A frame its reader fills with silence closes the stream at once (the frame is zero either way, so w may jump there; a fade-out under way ends there as if its last frame had been rendered), and the first frame the reader copies while the stream is closed starts a fade-in, with one exception. While a stop, a delay change or a held correction waits (S185, S187, S188), the stream stays closed after its fade-out until the read that applies the change: every frame is rendered as an exact zero, copied frames included (also those after the fade-out's last frame in the same block), and none of them starts a fade-in. For a delay change or a correction, the first frame copied by the read that applies it (or by the next read, should no correction be due by then) starts the fade-in; after a stop the stream stays closed until the output stops. Without the exception the stream would fade in on the old timeline and jump when the change lands, mid-fade-in: about 1 % of the signal with 512-frame buffers, up to the full level with 4096-frame buffers, against the 0.16 % S191 allows. So an output fades in on the first audio of a session (S45), on the first audio of an output switched on or plugged in during a session (S117, S137, S161), whether its first read primes at once or after silent reads, after the masked silence of a delay increase or a step back (S44 step 3, S47, S52), and after a dry or starved read (S53, S54). For this, each read reports, besides filling its block, how many frames at the block's start it filled with silence and how many it then copied (S44 step 3 orders a block as silence, then copies, then silence; a copy the post-copy check discards, S40, counts as silence). The fade starts where the first copied frame leaves the converter: the device frame its position converts to, plus the converter's fixed latency at the device rate, rounded up; the frames before it stay closed and render as exact zeros. At ratio 1 that is exactly the first copied frame; at other rates it may start the fade up to a frame late (inaudible, S182). Reason: at each of these moments the output jumps from silence to whatever the source plays at that instant, up to full scale; this is the click heard on every start and source change, and on a speaker switched on mid-session. A source that starts from silence by itself loses nothing audible, and a note that begins exactly then loses only 20 ms of its attack.
- **S185** *Required.* Fade-out before an output stops. When the host stops an output whose device still runs (a speaker switched off, S117, S161; every output at a session stop, S186), the renderer fades out over its next F device frames, starting with the first callback that begins after the request, and renders exact zeros after them. It keeps rendering exact zeros after the fade-out and counts as *faded* only once the fade-out's last frame has been played; only then is its device's IO stopped (`stop`, S61). A device plays a frame well after the callback that rendered it: Core Audio renders about one buffer plus the device's safety offset ahead of the output, and WASAPI keeps up to a whole endpoint buffer queued, so stopping the IO as soon as the fade has been rendered, or one buffer later, drops the end of the fade and cuts the sound mid-waveform after all. On the Mac, the renderer publishes the host time at which the fade's last frame is output: its callback's timestamp (the output time of the buffer's first frame) plus that frame's offset in the buffer at the device rate, plain arithmetic on the audio thread. The control thread adds the device's and its output stream's reported latency (`kAudioDevicePropertyLatency`, `kAudioStreamPropertyLatency`, Apple's Core Audio documentation; a Bluetooth device's within S231's window) and counts the output as faded once the host clock passes that time. On Windows, the render thread, whose loop reads the endpoint's padding (`GetCurrentPadding`) anyway, counts the frames it wrote after the fade's last frame and, while stopping, reads the stream's clock (`IAudioClock::GetPosition` over `GetFrequency`, at the device rate) against every frame it wrote since the start, the silent prefill included. The output is faded once the frames written after the fade cover what the clock says is written but not yet played, taken as no less than the padding (only zeros are still queued) and no more than the padding plus 100 ms. Without a clock reading the rule is the padding plus the stream latency the endpoint reports (`IAudioClient::GetStreamLatency`). Reasons: `GetStreamLatency` read 0 on every endpoint of a Windows 11 PC, while the clock put the played position 4 to 30 ms past the queued buffer on the same endpoints (USB headphones about 29 ms), so the old rule could cut that much of the fade's end; the 100 ms bound keeps a clock that stalls, or a driver that counts more than the endpoint's own pipeline, from holding a stop for long, well inside S186's 500 ms. An output whose gain has been 0 for longer than that latency MAY count as faded at once, since nothing it still has queued is audible. An output whose device is gone or whose IO stopped by itself (S68) is stopped without a fade, since nothing can be rendered to it. This replaces the Windows app's fade over one device callback, whose length depended on how many frames that callback held.
- **S186** *Required.* Session stop and source change. `stop()` (S115) first asks every running renderer to fade out (S185) and the AirPlay inputs to fade out (S189), all at once, so any number of outputs costs one fade. It then waits until every renderer is faded, with capture still running: an output at a short hold-back fades out on frames that are still being captured (S42), and with capture stopped first its reader would run dry and cut (S53). Then it goes on as S115 says: capture stops, every renderer is stopped and removed, and the FIFO feed stops. Each renderer is faded within up to one device buffer (until its first faded callback begins), then F, then its output latency (S185: on the Mac about one buffer plus the safety offset plus the device's and stream's latency; on Windows what the endpoint's clock has queued, at most the endpoint buffer plus 100 ms) of the request: about 45 ms for built-in speakers with 512-frame buffers at 48 kHz (10.7 ms until the first faded callback, 20 ms of fade, then 10.7 ms of buffer and a few ms of safety offset and latency), and up to about 350 ms for a Bluetooth speaker, which sounds some 150 to 300 ms late (S172). The host waits for the slowest renderer but never more than 500 ms in all, which covers that Bluetooth range with room for larger buffers; an output that isn't faded by then (its IO stalled, or its latency is beyond the cap) is stopped anyway. A stop on a Bluetooth speaker thus takes about as long as the speaker's own lag, during which it is still playing the end of the audio anyway. The wait happens on the control thread, never on an audio thread or the FIFO writer (S190). Removing outputs while the session goes on (S117, S161) asks every output being removed to fade out at once and then waits once, the same way, for those outputs. A source change (S141, S163) stops and starts the capture under the speakers, so every local output and the AirPlay input fade out on the old source and fade in on the new one at its first audio (S184), with silence between them for as long as the rebuild and each output's hold-back take. There is no crossfade: the two sources are unrelated audio, and a crossfade would need both captures running at once, possibly at different rates. A stop after an interruption (S20, S21), the stop at quit (S110, within its 5 s budget) and the stop at an exit signal (S111, within its 4 s bound) run the same way; when capture has already stopped delivering, readers at a short hold-back are dry and their fades run over silence. This supersedes S115 in part: the outputs and the AirPlay inputs fade out before capture stops.
- **S187** *Required.* Delay and hold-back changes. A new delay for a running output (S66, and every re-timing of S177, which happens whenever an output joins or leaves, an offset or the reference changes, the AirPlay condition flips or L changes, S178) is applied between a fade-out and a fade-in. The renderer fades out at its current delay, keeping its device running, and only after the fade-out's last frame stores the new delay and asks its reader to resync (S46), so the reader still lands exactly on the new delay (`lagFrames = De − N`, S46, S56). The audio after the resync fades in (S184): after an increase once the masked silence of S47 has passed (still exactly Δ frames; above 5 s, see large increases below), after a decrease (S48) right after the skip. A change therefore applies up to F plus one device buffer later than S66 says, and a newer change that arrives during the fade-out replaces the waiting one without restarting the fade. A change that arrives while the renderer is closed (no audio yet, or between a fade-out and the next fade-in) goes to the reader at once, with no fade. This supersedes S66 in part: the change reaches the reader after the fade-out, not on the next callback. Reason: a skip forward joins two unrelated points of the waveform and an increase cuts to silence, both a click on every re-timing; under S177 that includes the other outputs whenever a speaker that sets the base is switched on or off. *Large increases* (2026-10-21; this supersedes S47 and S49 in part, for a running output's renderer): when a running output's renderer gives its reader a delay more than 5000 ms (at the capture rate, S3) above the one it gave it last (a change as above, or its first delay after the start), it also resets the reader's prime (S159). The reader then plays at once at the new delay, exactly as an output started there would (S45): it anchors at `W − De` on its first read once more than De frames are written, playing again the frames it already played in the last Δ, instead of the Δ of silence of S47. Before that much is written (a session younger than the new delay) it stays silent until it is, as a new output would. The fade-out before the change and the fade-in after it stay as above, and increases of up to 5000 ms and all decreases are unchanged (S47, S48). Reason: on the Mac a 15 or 20 s delay entered on a running speaker kept it silent for up to 20 s, which reads as an output that stopped, while switching the speaker off and on played it at once; 5 s lies above the usual AirPlay latency L (about 2 s, S178) that every local output gains when an AirPlay speaker is chosen, so that change usually still waits in silence. L may reach 20 000 ms (S178): with an AirPlay delay above 5 s, choosing an AirPlay speaker restarts every local speaker's reader, and each replays up to L of what it just played, as an output started fresh at its new hold-back would.
- **S188** *Required.* Corrections the reader makes by itself. A renderer reads with drift corrections *held*: a read that would make a drift correction (the *Drift* case of S44 step 2: a skip forward, S51, or a step back, S52, S53) leaves P alone, emits its block contiguously as if the error were inside the dead band, and reports that a correction is due. The renderer then fades out from the start of this block, over it and the next ones, still holding, and its first read after the fade-out's last frame is made without the hold, so the correction is made then, against that read's W and exactly as S51 and S52 say; the audio after it fades in (S184). Holding changes only the Drift case: prime, resync and dry reads (S44 step 2) and the masking of step 3 stay as they are, and the counters (S158) count a correction when it is made. A held correction comes up to F plus one block later, during which the error can grow by as much again (a step back while the writer has stopped moves e by N per read). So for a renderer, with F_c = round(0.020 × Rc), the worst deviation of S57 grows from T + N to T + 2N + F_c (4384 frames with 512-frame blocks at 48 kHz; 3664 at the suite's former T of 1680), and in a writer stall its reader never reaches W (S53) only when De > T + 2N + F_c, where S53 says T + N. The ring buffer suite's reader checks read without holding and are unchanged. Two edges stay unfaded: an overrun (S55; the frames a fade-out would play are already overwritten) and the start of an underrun (S53; the reader can't know that the writer stopped before it reaches W, and predicting it would cost every output a block of latency). Both are rare in normal play (a source that pauses usually fades out by itself), and the audio after them fades in (S184).
- **S189** *Required.* The AirPlay leg. Both AirPlay senders' inputs fade too: the FIFO feed (S84) for the bundled engine, and the native sender's input, which takes the same blocks after the equalizer (AirPlay spec P111, P143). Each applies the fade itself to the frames it is given, after the split of S7, so the ring and the AirPlay leg still get bit-identical input. Fade-in: in the FIFO writer over the first F frames it writes after each open of the pipe (S89, S169), which covers every session start (the writer opens the pipe after each `startWriter()`) and every engine restart (a new stream that would otherwise begin mid-waveform); in the native input over the first F frames after each rate set at a capture build (S141). Fade-out: in `feed` and in the native input, over the F frames given after a stop request (S186), then zeros until capture stops. A stop request closes `feed` only until the next session: its `setInputRate` and `startWriter()` (S94, S88) open it again, so the new session's audio passes, faded in by the writer after its open; the native input likewise opens again at its next rate set (S141) and fades in from there. F counts frames at the rate where the fade is applied: 48 000 Hz in the FIFO writer (S83, rounding as S86), Rc in `feed` and in the native input. Nothing in the bundled engine's documented interface or in the AirPlay specification promises a ramp at the start of a stream, so AudioSync doesn't rely on one; a fade costs one multiply per sample. What the speakers do when the stream is cut is not AudioSync's to shape: a session stop stops the engine's player at once (S99) and the native sender flushes or tears down (P100, P129), which drops the audio the receivers hold (about L), so the faded end of the stream is heard only when the engine plays the tail, for example while a switch-on holds the write queue (S101). Whether a receiver clicks when it is stopped or flushed mid-stream is a check on real receivers (test map). Holding a stop back by L so that the faded tail plays would make every stop about two seconds slower, and is not done; a source change sends the native sender's speakers no FLUSH (P143), so they play its faded tail. A backlog skip (S90) is not faded either: the frames before it are already in the pipe, and a fade-in after it alone would not remove the step.
- **S190** *Required.* Real time. A fade's state (per renderer, per AirPlay input, and in the FIFO writer) is allocated before the audio threads start (S128); w is computed per frame, or read from a table filled before the audio thread starts, with no allocation, lock, system call or logging (S126, S129), in O(N) per callback (S127). The control thread's requests are atomics that the audio thread takes at the start of a callback (a fade-out requested; a delay change waiting for its fade-out, its value stored before its flag as S133 says for a resync); a renderer publishes the output time of its fade's last frame (Mac) or that it is faded (Windows, S185) with a release store that the control thread reads with acquire. The only wait is the control thread's in S186, bounded at 500 ms.
- **S191** *Required.* What "no click" means, as the tests check it. With a constant input of amplitude A (|A| ≤ 1) at gain g on a stream whose fade length is F: (a) frame i of a fade is A × g × w(i) with S182's w, within 1e−6 (within one 16-bit step on the pipe); (b) across every boundary of S184 to S189 other than the unfaded edges they name (a lost device, an output stopped at the 500 ms cap, an overrun, the start of an underrun, a backlog skip, a receiver stopped or flushed), from the last frame before a fade-out to the first frame after the next fade-in, silence between them included, no two consecutive frames differ by more than A × g × π / (2F) plus that tolerance, where a hard edge differs by up to A × g; (c) every frame after a fade-out's last one is exactly 0 until the next fade-in starts; (d) with a sine of amplitude A instead, at any frequency, no step between consecutive frames across those boundaries exceeds the sine's own largest step plus A × g × π / (2F). For timing: (e) `stop()` returns within S186's bound (up to one buffer, F and the output latency of the slowest output, never more than 500 ms), and no output's IO stops before its fade-out's last frame has played unless the cap passed, which a simulated device that plays each buffer one buffer plus a safety offset after its callback must show (a check that only looks at rendered frames can't); (f) a delay change lands exactly on the new delay (S46) once its fade-out is done.

## 7. Volume

- **S70** *Observed* (`Volume.swift:8-16`; tests "volume: …"). Slider position p (0…100) maps to linear gain `g(p) = (clamp(p, 0, 100) / 100)³`; the inverse is `p(g) = 100 × cbrt(clamp(g, 0, 1))`. The curve is strictly increasing over integer positions. Reference values:

  | p | 0 | 1 | 10 | 25 | 50 | 60 | 75 | 90 | 100 |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | g | 0 | 1e−6 | 0.001 | 0.015625 | 0.125 | 0.216 | 0.421875 | 0.729 | 1 |
  | dB | −∞ | −120.00 | −60.00 | −36.12 | −18.06 | −13.31 | −7.50 | −2.75 | 0.00 |

- **S71** *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift`, `effectiveGain`; `AppSettings.swift`, `defaultVolume`); *Required* for zones (S249; `core/src/volume.rs`, `zoned_output_gain`; `asc_zone_output_gain`; `desktop/src/audio/outputs.rs`, `OutputPolicy::params`). A local output's renderer gain is `g(local p) × g(zone p) × g(master p)`, zone p being 100 when the output is in no zone. New devices start at local p = 60 (≈ −13.3 dB); the master and a new zone start at 100.
- **S72** *Retired in part with the bundled engine (2026-10-20)*: a master change still re-applies the gain of every active local output (S71); what reaches the AirPlay speakers is the AirPlay sender's (`docs/AIRPLAY_SPEC.md`), and the rest is a record. *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:47-52`, `setMasterVolume`; `SpeakerState.swift`, `LocalOutputSync.reapplyGains`; test "S72 master: every running local speaker gets its gain again, and only those"). A master change re-applies the gain of every active local output, and of no other. *Record* (until 2026-10-20: `AirPlayReconcile.swift:395-404`, deleted with the bundled engine; tests "volume: moving the master scales each playing speaker from its own volume, never above it", "volume: a master move while stopped sends nothing, and the next start carries it", removed with it): while streaming, a master change also sent every enabled, listed AirPlay speaker its own volume times the new master (S105), never more than its own and without changing it; while stopped it sent nothing, and the next start carried the master. The engine's master volume was never written (the suites failed if any source sent `PUT /api/player/volume` or a 'pvol' metadata item): the engine's master was its loudest selected speaker's volume, and a master write rescaled every selected speaker relative to it, so it would have set a lone speaker to the master position.
- **S73** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:58-62`; `AirPlayReconcile.swift:372-385`; engine client `:156-161`; tests "volume: an AirPlay speaker plays at its own volume times the master, never above its own", "volume: after a master move the poll keeps the speaker's own volume"). An AirPlay speaker's own volume (its slider, 0–100, saved across launches) reaches the engine per output as the integer `round(own × master / 100)` on the engine's own 0–100 scale (linear, not the cubic curve), the AirPlay counterpart of S71, so never above the speaker's own volume. A slider change sends that value at once. The volume the engine reports never replaces a speaker's own volume, except for a speaker without one (S105).
- **S74** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`Volume.swift:19-36`; engine client `:156-172`; tests "volume writes: …"). Volume requests are coalesced per output: storing a value when none is waiting queues one flush; later values replace the waiting one; the flush sends the latest value once; targets never overwrite each other.
- **S75** *Observed* (`AppSettings.swift:44-46`, `:109-113`, `:160-169`; "volume: legacy 3.49% migrates to ≈33", "settings: older linear gains become slider positions (3.49 % → 32.68, 150 % → 100, S75)"). Legacy linear percentages (0…150) migrate as `p(pct / 100)`, clamped (3.49 % → 32.68; anything ≥ 100 % → 100). Local volumes stored that way are converted once at launch, saved as positions and the old values removed; positions already stored win.
- **S76** *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:51-62`). Host-side volume inputs are clamped to [0, 100] before use.

### Output devices' own volume

S192 to S202 make a turned-down or muted output device visible. Every local output also has its own volume and mute, kept by the operating system per device and applied after AudioSync's renderer gain (S71), so what is heard is AudioSync's gain times the device's. On a Mac only the default output's volume is in the Sound menu and on the volume keys; the others are reached only through Audio MIDI Setup, so a device left at a low volume stays out of sight: at a test on a MacBook, its speakers sounded very quiet with AudioSync's slider at 100 because their own volume was 13 %. Windows keeps a volume and a mute per endpoint the same way. Before these clauses neither app read or set an output device's volume or mute (the Windows app read them only for the double-audio check, `desktop/src/audio/wasapi/routing.rs`); both apps follow them now (`desktop/src/audio/controller/device_volume.rs` on Windows, `Sources/AudioSync/DeviceVolume.swift` on the Mac). These clauses are design requirements, kept small for the feature freeze: AudioSync reads and shows, and writes only when the user clicks the fix.

- **S192** *Required.* Scope. The *device volume* of a local output is its operating system volume as a scalar from 0 to 1 (the position of the system's own slider for that device) and its mute. AudioSync reads both for every local output row that is switched on, whether or not a session runs, and for no other device: not for AirPlay speakers (whose volume is S73), not for outputs switched off, not for the capture source. It never sets a device volume or mute by itself: not at launch, at a start or stop, when an output is switched on or plugged in, on a source change, at quit, nor to restore an earlier value. The only write is the fix the user clicks (S197). The device volume never changes AudioSync's own volumes, its stored settings or the renderer gain (S70 to S76), and has no part in delay or sync.
- **S193** *Required.* Reading on the Mac (Apple's Core Audio documentation). In the output scope of the device: the volume is `kAudioDevicePropertyVolumeScalar` of the main element when the device has it there; otherwise the mean of that property over the device's preferred stereo channels (`kAudioDevicePropertyPreferredChannelsForStereo`, channels 1 and 2 when the device doesn't say) that have it. The mute is `kAudioDevicePropertyMute` of the main element when the device has it; otherwise the device counts as muted only when every one of those channels that has a mute is muted. Each part counts as *known* only when its property is present and settable (`AudioObjectIsPropertySettable`): a volume that can't be set can't be fixed, so it is treated as absent. Many HDMI and DisplayPort outputs, some USB devices, and aggregate and multi-output devices have neither; S196 then shows nothing. Reading happens on the control or main thread, never on an audio thread.
- **S194** *Required.* Reading on Windows. The volume is `IAudioEndpointVolume::GetMasterVolumeLevelScalar` of the endpoint and the mute is `GetMute`, as the double-audio snapshot reads them today. Windows applies the endpoint volume in software where the hardware has none, so both parts are known whenever the endpoint's volume control can be activated and read; when activation or a read fails, both are unknown. Channel volumes are not used: the master scalar is what the Sound settings slider shows.
- **S195** *Required.* Following changes. When an output's row is switched on (including the rows switched on at launch) or its device appears again (S137, S161), the host reads its device volume once and registers for changes: on the Mac a property listener on each address S193 read (`AudioObjectAddPropertyListenerBlock`, on a serial queue, results passed to the main thread); on Windows `IAudioEndpointVolume::RegisterControlChangeNotify`, whose callback runs on a system thread and passes the new mute and level to the UI thread. The row then shows the latest value; several notifications before the UI thread runs collapse into one update. The host removes the listener or unregisters the callback when the row is switched off, its device goes away, or the app quits, and holds no listener for any other device. A notification never touches an audio thread or the renderer.
- **S196** *Required.* The decision, a pure function of the two parts. A known volume v becomes the whole percent `round(100 × clamp(v, 0, 1))` (half away from zero); a NaN or infinite reading makes the volume unknown. The output is *muted* when the mute is known and set, *low* when the volume is known and 25 % or less, both when both hold, and *fine* otherwise; with both parts unknown it is *unknown*. Muted, low, and muted and low show a hint (S198); fine and unknown show nothing. Reason for 25 %: it catches a device left near the bottom (13 % in the case above, which sounded very quiet at AudioSync's 100) and leaves alone users who keep a device moderately low on purpose, such as a laptop at a third of its volume at night. The bound counts as low itself, so a device the system shows at 25 % gets the hint, as "a quarter or less" reads. A volume of 0 % without mute counts as low. The hint stays for as long as the decision does: it has no dismiss button and doesn't time out, and it goes only when the volume or the mute changes (or the row is switched off). This is intended: a user who keeps headphones at 15 % on purpose keeps seeing it, and it is small enough to live with.
- **S197** *Required.* The fix. One click writes, once each: the volume, set to 0.75 only when the output is low; and the mute, cleared only when it is muted. The fix never lowers a volume, so unmuting a device at 90 % leaves it at 90 %. On the Mac it sets the same addresses S193 read: the main element's scalar, or each preferred stereo channel's scalar to 0.75 (a channel balance set on such a device is lost, which is rare and stated here), and the main mute or each channel's mute to 0; on Windows `SetMasterVolumeLevelScalar(0.75)` and `SetMute(false)`. The row is then updated from the change notification (S195), not from the values written, so a write the device ignores or refuses leaves the hint and the button exactly as they were, and only the log says so (S201). This is intended: the row always shows what the device reports, and a second click simply tries again. No dialog, alert or confirmation is shown. Reason for 75 % rather than 100 %: it is loud enough that AudioSync's own slider spans its useful range again, and short of the device's maximum, so one click doesn't put headphones or a nearby speaker at full scale; anyone who wants more raises it in the system's settings or with AudioSync's slider.
- **S198** *Required.* Where the hint shows. In the main window's row of a switched-on local output (Mac `SpeakerRow`, Windows `SpeakerRow` in `widgets.slint`), on its own line under the volume slider: a small speaker glyph (decorative, hidden from VoiceOver and Narrator), the hint text in the secondary text style with at least 4.5:1 contrast in light and dark appearance, and the fix as a borderless, link-style button after it. It appears and disappears with the decision (S196) without moving focus, raising a window or showing a notice, and never on an AirPlay row or a row switched off; a click on the fix while it has keyboard focus first moves focus to the row's volume slider, so focus doesn't vanish with the button once the fix works. The Mac's compact menu bar panel (`CompactRow`) and the Windows tray flyout (`FlyoutRow`) have no per-output slider, so their row of a switched-on local output shows the state as a small glyph only, in the slot at the row's right where the availability note sits (the platform's mute glyph when muted, on the Mac a speaker with a slash and on Windows the Segoe Fluent Icons Mute glyph, a speaker with a cross; a speaker with one wave when low), with no button: the hint text of S199 is its tooltip on the Mac and is added to the row's VoiceOver hint (Mac) or accessible description (Windows), and the fix is in the main window. The glyph uses the secondary text color, not a warning color, so it reads as information. On the Mac the main window's hint text carries a tooltip that explains it (S199).
- **S199** *Required.* Wording, through `UIStrings` on the Mac and `strings.slint` on Windows; n is the whole percent of S196:

  | Case | Mac hint | Mac button | Windows hint | Windows button |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | low | macOS volume is n% | Set to 75% | Windows volume is n% | Set to 75% |
  | muted | Muted in macOS | Unmute | Muted in Windows | Unmute |
  | muted and low | Muted in macOS, volume n% | Unmute at 75% | Muted in Windows, volume n% | Unmute at 75% |

  The Mac tooltip: "macOS has its own volume for each output, and it limits AudioSync's slider." Every percentage, in the hints and in the button labels ("Set to 75%", "Unmute at 75%") alike, follows its platform: the Mac formats it for the user's locale ("13 %" in French), the Windows app writes "13%" in every locale, like its other numbers.
- **S200** *Required.* Accessibility. The hint text is one static-text element whose label names the output: "MacBook Pro Speakers: macOS volume is 13%", "MacBook Pro Speakers: muted in macOS", "MacBook Pro Speakers: muted in macOS, volume 13%" (on Windows "…: Windows volume is 13%" and so on). The fix button's label starts with its visible text, so Voice Control and speech input find it by what is on screen, and then names the output: "Set to 75%, MacBook Pro Speakers", "Unmute, MacBook Pro Speakers", "Unmute at 75%, MacBook Pro Speakers" (the same on Windows); that it is the macOS or Windows volume is said by the hint read just before it. VoiceOver and Narrator reach both in reading order right after the row's volume slider; the slider's own label and value are unchanged. The hint appearing is not announced, so a system volume being dragged elsewhere doesn't interrupt a screen reader.
- **S201** *Required.* Debug log (the Mac's `AUDIOSYNC_DEBUG` log, the Windows debug log). One line when an output's device volume is first read after S195 registers it (the output as the log already names outputs, the percent or "unknown", muted or not, and which parts are settable); one line when the decision of S196 changes (for example "fine → low"); and one line per fix click with what was written and whether each write succeeded. A reading whose decision stays the same logs nothing, so dragging a system slider from 80 % to 30 % logs nothing and dragging it on to 20 % logs one line. Nothing is logged from an audio thread or from the listener callback itself; the line is written where the UI thread applies the reading.
- **S202** *Required.* Outputs that change underneath. A device that goes away drops its listener and its hint (S195); when it comes back its device volume is read afresh, and so is a row switched off and on again. Every notification re-reads both parts the way S193 or S194 says rather than trusting the value it carries alone, so a device whose controls changed is read as it is now. Reading stays best-effort: an unreadable part counts as unknown, which shows nothing rather than a wrong hint.

## 8. AirPlay sync rule (local delay policy)

- **S77** *Superseded by S175 and S178* (local delay model, below). *Observed* (`AppSettings.swift:41-43`, `:54-55`, `:81-82`, `:106-107`; `AppDelegate.swift:129-130`; `AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:100-106`; `README.md` "Using it"). Each local output has a configured delay in ms, range 0…20 000, default 2000 (seeded from a legacy single delay when present, clamped to the range). Non-finite input is ignored; other input is clamped.
- **S78** *Superseded by S176.* *Observed* (`SpeakerState.swift:49-52`; tests "delay: AirPlay chosen → configured delay", "delay: no AirPlay → plays at once", "delay: zero stays zero"). Effective delay = the configured delay while an AirPlay speaker is chosen, otherwise 0. The configured value is kept either way. *Now covered by the core.*
- **S79** *Observed* (`SpeakerState.swift:54-58`; tests "delay: a chosen speaker not listed today doesn't count", "delay: chosen and listed counts", "delay: listed but not chosen doesn't count"). "An AirPlay speaker is chosen" means at least one AirPlay output ID is both enabled by the user and present in the AirPlay engine's latest output list (this Mac's own receiver is never in that list, S100). *Now covered by the core.*
- **S80** *Superseded by S177.* *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:110-136`; `AppDelegate.swift:83-88`, `:136-138`). Whenever that condition flips (the enabled set or the listed set changed), every running local output gets its new effective delay live through S66: a flip to 0 skips forward (S48), a flip back inserts silence (S47).
- **S81** *Observed* (`AppDelegate+AirPlay.swift:24-29`). A poll that finds the AirPlay engine unreachable leaves the listed set unchanged, so an engine restart doesn't flap local delays.

### Local delay model (offsets relative to a reference output)

S172 to S181 replace the rule that local outputs play at once unless an AirPlay speaker is chosen (S77, S78, S80). Under that rule the delay fields did nothing with only local outputs, although a Bluetooth speaker sounds some 150 to 300 ms behind a wired one, and every row, the built-in speakers included, carried an absolute delay of about two seconds that only meant something next to AirPlay. In this model the built-in speakers are the reference and have no delay setting; every other local output has an offset relative to them that always applies; the host turns the offsets of the outputs taking part into hold-backs and adds the AirPlay latency while an AirPlay speaker is chosen. The Rust core, the Mac app and the Windows app follow these clauses; builds before them behave as the superseded clauses say. S79 (what "an AirPlay speaker is chosen" means) and S81 still apply.

- **S172** *Required.* Terms used by S173 to S181:
  - A *local output* is an output the host renders to itself (S60), keyed by device UID on the Mac and by endpoint ID on Windows.
  - The *reference* R is at most one local output, chosen by S173 on the Mac and by S174 on Windows. It defines time 0 and has no delay setting of its own.
  - The *offset* o(x) of every other local output x is how much longer, in ms, x is held back than the reference: positive for an output that sounds early next to the reference (or that the user wants later), negative for one that sounds late (a Bluetooth speaker is typically 150 to 300 ms late, so about −150 to −300). The reference's offset is 0 by definition, whatever value may be stored for that output.
  - The outputs *taking part*, T, are the local outputs the user switched on whose devices are listed now (enabled ∩ available, S161), the reference included when it is one of them. An enabled output whose device is missing (unplugged, a Bluetooth device out of range) takes part again once its device is listed.
  - The *hold-back* h(x) is the delay in ms the host gives the renderer of x (`attachOutput` and `setDelay`, S61, S112), what S78, S117, S137 and S144 call the effective or configured delay a renderer gets. Hold-backs are computed by S176; they are never stored or shown.
- **S173** *Required.* Mac reference. The candidates are the listed output devices (S171) whose transport type is built-in (`kAudioDeviceTransportTypeBuiltIn`, Apple's Core Audio documentation). The reference is the first candidate in Core Audio order whose first output stream reports the speaker terminal type (`kAudioStreamTerminalTypeSpeaker` for `kAudioStreamPropertyTerminalType`); when no candidate reports it (the property can't be read, or reports another type, as an Intel Mac's single built-in device may while its headphone jack is in use), the first candidate in Core Audio order; with no candidate, there is no reference. So on an Apple silicon MacBook the built-in speakers stay the reference while the built-in headphone output, also of transport type built-in, is listed beside them, and a Mac mini, iMac, Mac Studio or Mac Pro uses its own built-in speaker. A built-in output that isn't the reference (that headphone output) has an offset like any other output. The choice is made afresh from every device list and doesn't depend on whether the reference is switched on: switched off, it is still the reference (its row still has no delay field, S181, and the other offsets keep their meaning), it just doesn't take part (S176). With no reference, every local output has an offset, and the offsets are relative to each other only (S176 needs nothing more).
- **S174** *Required.* Windows reference. The reference is the listed output of kind BuiltIn, the PC's own audio hardware (an endpoint whose form factor is Speakers or LineLevel and whose device enumerator is not USB, Bluetooth or ROOT, as `output_kind` in `desktop/src/audio/outputs.rs` decides), with the smallest endpoint ID in byte order (endpoint IDs are ASCII); with no BuiltIn output listed, there is no reference, as in S173. Reasons: BuiltIn is the Windows counterpart of the Mac's built-in speakers, the onboard codec with the shortest and steadiest output latency (a laptop's speakers, a desktop's line out); headphones on the PC's own jack are of kind Headphones and have an offset like any other output, usually 0; endpoint IDs stay the same across restarts and reboots, so on a PC with more than one BuiltIn endpoint the reference doesn't move between launches; Windows' default output is not used, because the user changes it freely and every offset would change meaning with it. Switched off, the reference stays the reference, as in S173.
- **S175** *Required.* Offsets. An offset is a finite number of ms from −2000 to +20 000; an output that never had one set has offset 0. Input that isn't finite is ignored and other input is clamped to that range (for local outputs this replaces the 0 to 20 000 ms of S77 and S124). Offsets are stored per output (device UID or endpoint ID) and kept across launches whether the output is connected, switched on or the reference at the moment; a stored offset of the reference is kept but not used while it is the reference (S172). The window edits whole ms (S181). The lower bound covers Bluetooth and other wireless outputs that sound up to two seconds late; the upper bound is S77's maximum.
- **S176** *Required.* Hold-backs. With o′(x) = 0 for the reference and o(x) for every other output, the base is
  `m = min({ o′(y) : y ∈ T } ∪ { −L, only while an AirPlay speaker is chosen (S79) })`,
  and every x in T gets `h(x) = min(o′(x) − m, 20 000)` ms, with L from S178. No hold-back is negative. Without AirPlay, the output with the smallest offset among those taking part plays at once (at the session lead, S208) and the others wait for it by exactly the difference (S208). Adding the same number to every offset therefore changes nothing, and a lone output plays at once whatever its offset, since there is nothing to line it up with. With an AirPlay speaker chosen, in the usual case where no offset in T is below −L (L is about two seconds, a Bluetooth offset a few hundred ms), m = −L and h(x) = L + o′(x): the reference sounds when the AirPlay speakers are taken to sound (S178) and every other output by its offset. An output taking part whose offset is below −L sounds later than the AirPlay speakers themselves; it then sets m, the local outputs still line up with each other, and the AirPlay speakers lead them by −m − L ms, because AudioSync never holds the AirPlay path back (S8); under the native sender, raising every AirPlay speaker's d_s by that much, where the window offers d_s, makes up for it. The cap at 20 000 ms (S77's maximum, which the ring covers, S36) only bites when a large offset meets a large L or a negative base; that output then plays early by the excess. The source makes no difference: under live input too the local outputs wait L (S244). Examples with the built-in speakers R, a USB speaker at +150, a Bluetooth speaker at −250 and L = 2000 (hold-backs in ms):

  | Taking part | AirPlay chosen | m | h(R) | h(USB) | h(Bluetooth) |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | R, USB | no | 0 | 0 | 150 | – |
  | R, Bluetooth | no | −250 | 250 | – | 0 |
  | R, USB, Bluetooth | no | −250 | 250 | 400 | 0 |
  | USB, Bluetooth (R switched off) | no | −250 | – | 400 | 0 |
  | Bluetooth alone | no | −250 | – | – | 0 |
  | R, USB, Bluetooth | yes | −2000 | 2000 | 2150 | 1750 |
  | Bluetooth alone | yes | −2000 | – | – | 1750 |

  The first row is the reported bug: under S78, with no AirPlay speaker chosen, the USB speaker plays at 0 like the built-in speakers and its delay does nothing.
- **S177** *Required.* Re-timing. The host recomputes every hold-back whenever T changes (a speaker switched on or off, a device listed again or lost, S161, S68), the offset of an output taking part changes, the reference changes (S173, S174), the AirPlay condition flips (S79), or L changes (S178). Each running renderer whose hold-back changed gets the new one through `setDelay` (S66): an increase inserts exactly that much silence (S47), a decrease skips forward (S48), and nothing is ever played twice (S49); an increase of more than 5 s instead restarts the reader and plays at once at the new hold-back, replaying up to that increase (S187, large increases). A renderer whose hold-back is unchanged gets no call, so it gets no resync. An output being attached starts at its current hold-back (S117, S45). When m changes, the hold-back of every output already taking part whose own offset didn't change moves by that same change: a Bluetooth speaker at −250 switched on next to the built-in speakers and a USB speaker at +150 makes those two wait 250 ms longer (250 ms of silence each, then in step with it), and switched off again, they skip 250 ms forward; editing that Bluetooth speaker's offset to −300 leaves its own hold-back at 0 and moves the other two 50 ms later. A poll that finds the AirPlay engine unreachable leaves the listed set as it was (S81), so it re-times nothing. This replaces S80, where only the AirPlay flip re-timed outputs and every hold-back went to 0 or came back from it. A switch of the source changes no hold-back (S244).
- **S244** *Required* (rewritten 2026-10-24; Windows test "live_input_waits_for_an_airplay_speaker_like_any_source"). Live input changes no hold-back. While the source is an input device (live input), every output plays as under the system mix or an app's audio: with a network speaker playing (an AirPlay speaker chosen, S178, or a Cast speaker on), the local outputs wait for it by L or the Cast speaker's wait, whichever is longer (S176), so the network speakers and the local outputs stay in step, and the sound reaches every speaker about L after the input; with no network speaker playing, the local outputs play at the session lead (S208) and line up with each other by their offsets alone, as under any source. A switch of the source to or from live input re-times nothing (S177). Calibration does not depend on the source either: the offsets and the AirPlay delay measured on music hold for live input. Reason: with a network speaker playing, that speaker sounds about L after the input whether or not the local outputs wait, so a live voice or instrument can't be heard in step with it in the room either way; a line-in source that is silent in the room (a turntable, a phone, a console) needs the speakers in step with each other, which only the wait gives. This replaces the earlier S244 (2026-10-23), under which a local output skipped the network wait for live input and the network speakers then sounded about L after the local outputs.
- **S178** *Required.* The AirPlay latency L of S176, the time from a frame's arrival to the moment the AirPlay speakers are taken to sound it, depends on the AirPlay sender and only counts while an AirPlay speaker is chosen. Each sender has one stored host setting for it, kept across launches, which the window shows as the *AirPlay delay* (S181); the user tunes it to line the reference up with the AirPlay speakers. Input that isn't finite is ignored and other input is clamped.
  - *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Bundled engine (the Mac's AirPlay sender before AudioSync's own).* The engine reports no latency, so the setting is absolute: L is the stored AirPlay delay, 0 to 20 000 ms, 2000 by default (S77's default; `README.md`: AirPlay speakers play about two seconds late). It takes over the role the reference's own delay had under S77 (S179). In the usual case h(x) = L + o′(x), which is S78's "configured delay" with configured = L + offset.
  - *Native sender* (AirPlay spec P98, P113, P114). *Superseded in part by S283*: the host uses L_s + E wherever this bullet uses L_s. The sender reports its base latency L_s, counted on the sender clock from c(n) as P114 says (before its first report, the configured Lf, P98, 1750 ms by default), and the setting is an adjustment A on top of it: −2000 to +20 000 ms, 0 by default, with `L = clamp(L_s + A, 0, 20 000)`. A is relative, so it stays right when L_s changes; no absolute latency is ever stored for this sender, since it would go stale with the next Lf change or timeline restart. A covers receivers that sound later (or earlier) than L_s says. This replaces P114's L + d_local with d_local = clamp(configured − L, 0, 3000): the trim is now the offset, which may be negative down to −L and isn't capped at 3000 ms (only h is capped, S176), and P114's one-time migration is replaced by S179 and S180. An AirPlay speaker's own delay d_s (P99) is unaffected and still moves that speaker alone.
  - *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* The two settings are separate. The Mac, which could use either sender, kept both, and switching senders switched settings; neither was ever converted into the other. The Mac now reads only the native sender's adjustment; S179 still stores the bundled engine's value once, for older builds.
  - A change of L (the user edits the AirPlay delay; the native sender reports a new L_s after an Lf change or a timeline restart, P98, P143) re-times every output taking part (S177).
- **S280** *Required* (AirPlay spec P199 to P202). The session's AirPlay latency, on both hosts. The host keeps, per AirPlay speaker, the latest R_s the crate's latency events reported in this run (in memory, keyed by receiver ID, never stored, dropped when the speaker leaves discovery), and computes Lf with the core's one function (`asc_airplay_session_latency_frames`, P200) over the speakers that are in the stream or being added to it, every member of a target included. It calls `asc_airplay_set_latency` with the result whenever the result changes: before adding speakers to a stream that has none, at every latency event, and when a speaker leaves the stream; the crate decides when the value takes effect (P201). One latency for all AirPlay speakers stays the rule (S178, S237): R_s never changes the stored d_s or the AirPlay delay A. It is the speaker's uncalibrated starting point instead, a separate per-speaker term that S283 adds on top of d_s, and calibration measures relative to it.
- **S281** *Required.* A new L and the local outputs. When the crate's timeline event reports a new L_s, S178 computes L from it and the unchanged adjustment A, and S177 re-times every output taking part, as for any change of L; the network wait of S176 and S244 takes the new L the same way (with a Cast speaker playing, the local outputs wait for the longer of L and its wait). A is relative to L_s and every d_s relative to L (P96), so an offset, the AirPlay delay and each speaker's delay measured by calibration (S237) stay right when a later session runs at another L_s, and the AirPlay delay field shows the new L. Since the crate changes Lf only before any AirPlay speaker sounds or when none plays (P201), the local outputs see no change of L while AirPlay speakers sound. A raise in a stream's first seconds that comes within L of the AirPlay switch-on lengthens the silence S177 inserted for that switch-on; a later one inserts one more silence of the difference, with S187's fades.
- **S282** *Required.* Debug log (S201's logs). One line per latency event: the speaker as the log names it, L + d_s, and "reports R ms (Audio-Latency, audioLatencies, arrivalToRender)" with the sources that gave a value, or "reports nothing"; one line when the host sets a new Lf or E (S283), with the values, each speaker's d′_s and the speakers they were computed over; one line when the crate holds a value as pending and when it applies it; the warnings of P200 and P201 as warnings. No line on the real-time path.
- **S283** *Required* (AirPlay spec P199, P200, P201). A receiver's reported latency as its arrival estimate. Each AirPlay speaker s in the stream has an estimate e_s = R_s in ms (0 when it reported nothing), kept in memory as S280 keeps R_s and never stored; the stored d_s and the AirPlay delay A keep their meaning and their stored values. With E the largest e_s among the AirPlay speakers in the stream, the host gives the sender, for each one, the delay `d′_s = min(d_s + E − e_s, 20 000)` ms in place of d_s (P99), and uses `L_s + E` wherever S178 and S181 use L_s, so `L = clamp(L_s + E + A, 0, 20 000)`. Every AirPlay speaker is then taken to sound at L_s + E + d_s after a frame's arrival, an uncalibrated receiver that reports 220 ms and adds it on top of the stated time sounds in step with the others within its own error rather than 220 ms late, and the local outputs wait for it through S176 and S244 unchanged, since they read L. Calibration stays relative on top: S235's expected arrival includes R_s, S237's differences are measured against it and move only A, and a Calibrate All after this change takes out whatever an AirPlay delay measured before it already held of R_s. E changes only when Lf may (P201): while no AirPlay speaker in the stream has reached its first-sound time (P115), or when the stream has no speaker; a capture switch doesn't apply it (S141, P143); otherwise it is held, and a speaker that joins with e_s above E plays e_s − E late, with one debug line, until then. A speaker that joins with e_s at or below E changes only its own d′_s. *Source:* AudioSync design (advisor decision of 2026-10-24); hardware trace (the television of AirPlay spec P203 measured 220 to 230 ms past L). *Required:* whether a receiver adds its reported latency is checked on hardware (AirPlay spec P203); one that doesn't plays e_s early until calibrated.
- **S284** *Required* (AirPlay spec P204, P208, P211; S280, S283). *Latency with several AirPlay 2 receivers.* S280 and S283 apply over every AirPlay speaker in the stream, whatever its path and timing protocol, so several AirPlay 2 receivers share one Lf and one E. A target stays one speaker for S283, as both hosts already treat it: its e_s is the largest R_s among its members, and it gets one d′_s = min(d_s + E − e_s, 20 000) from its one stored d_s, the same for every member (AirPlay spec P179). A target's e_s rises only when E may change; a member that joins later with a larger R_s plays the difference late until then. A receiver that joins while others sound enters Lf and E only when AirPlay spec P201 lets them change: with e_s at or below E only its own d′_s is computed; above E it plays e_s − E late, with S283's debug line, until the stream is empty.
