A receiver that leaves or fails changes neither Lf nor E while another AirPlay speaker sounds, so no remaining speaker's d′_s changes, none is flushed (AirPlay spec P100) and nothing is re-timed (S177); the next value is computed when the stream next has no speaker. Calibration (S237) measures each AirPlay 2 receiver on its own, as it does an AirPlay 1 speaker. *Source:* AudioSync design.
- **S285** *Required* (AirPlay spec P204, P211; S246, S252). *Several AirPlay 2 speakers in the hosts.* Once the sender drives several AirPlay 2 receivers (P204), every AirPlay 2 speaker and target the user switches on plays: S246's and S252's waits under P169 and the status "Waiting for the other AirPlay 2 speaker" apply only to a speaker the sender refused with "too many AirPlay 2 speakers", or with "PTP in use on another network" (AirPlay spec P205); either shows its reason on the speaker's row, counts as on and starts when the sender lets it. A failure of one AirPlay 2 speaker (a code or password prompt, S223; a wrong password; unreachable) shows on that speaker's row only, and the other speakers and the stream play on; a prompt never pauses the stream. Each AirPlay 2 speaker keeps its own volume, delay and calibration. The wording of both reasons goes through UIStrings on the Mac and strings.slint on Windows. *Source:* AudioSync design.
- **S179** *Required.* Mac migration, once. Before this model the Mac stored an absolute delay per device UID (S77, the `localDelayMsByUID` defaults key) that applied only while an AirPlay speaker was chosen, and played outputs without one at D0: the legacy single delay (the `delayMs` key) clamped to 0 to 20 000, else 2000. The first time a build with this model has both its stored values and a device list, it takes R from S173 and D_R, the reference's stored delay, or D0 when it has none or there is no reference, and stores:
  - the bundled engine's AirPlay delay (S178), L_new = D_R when D_R ≥ 1000 ms, a value tuned against AirPlay; else 2000 ms, since a smaller value can only have been meant to line local outputs up with each other;
  - for every other UID x with a stored delay D_x: o(x) = clamp(D_x − D_R, −2000, 20 000);
  - for every other UID without a stored delay that is switched on (the `enabledLocalUIDs` key) or in the device list of that moment (S171): o(x) = clamp(D0 − L_new, −2000, 20 000) when D0 ≥ 1000 ms, which keeps its D0 sound with AirPlay (with D_R ≥ 1000 that is D0 − D_R), and o(x) = clamp(D0 − D_R, −2000, 20 000) when D0 is below 1000 ms, which keeps its place relative to the reference (so untuned outputs that all played at D0 keep playing together, also when the reference is one of them); nothing for any other UID (offset 0);
  - the native sender's adjustment A (S178) = 0: L_s isn't known at this moment, and no stored delay was ever read for that sender except through P114's clamp;
  - nothing for R: its old delay became the AirPlay delay, or is dropped when below 1000 ms.

  The new values go under new keys (`localOffsetMsByUID`, `airPlayDelayMs`, `nativeAirPlayAdjustmentMs`), and the migration counts as done once a separate flag, `localDelayModelMigrated`, is set; it is written last, so a migration cut short runs again from the same old values. The old keys are left as they are, so an older build still finds its own values and a newer one never reads them again. Effect, with the bundled engine: with an AirPlay speaker chosen and D_R ≥ 1000, every output with a stored delay, and every switched-on or listed output that played at D0, plays exactly as before as long as its offset needed no clamping (h = D_R + D_x − D_R = D_x, with D_x = D0 for one without a stored delay, S176); without AirPlay, outputs now line up by their offsets where they all used to play at once. Under the native sender (the Mac's only one since 2026-10-20) the offsets apply with the reported L_s (S178) instead of P114's clamp. Worked examples, in ms, hold-backs listed in the order of the first column:

  | Stored before | D0 | Stored after | h with AirPlay chosen | h without AirPlay, all on |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | R 2000, USB 2100, Bluetooth 1800 | 2000 | AirPlay delay 2000; USB +100, Bluetooth −200 | 2000, 2100, 1800 (as before) | 200, 300, 0 (before: 0, 0, 0) |
  | R none, HDMI TV 2400 | 2000 | AirPlay delay 2000; TV +400 | 2000, 2400 (as before) | 0, 400 |
  | R 2300, USB none (listed) | 2000 | AirPlay delay 2300; USB −300 | 2300, 2000 (as before) | 300, 0 |
  | R none, USB none (listed) | 0 (legacy `delayMs` 0) | AirPlay delay 2000; USB 0 | 2000, 2000 (before: 0, 0, about two seconds ahead of AirPlay) | 0, 0 |
  | R 2500, Bluetooth 2250 | 2000 | AirPlay delay 2500; Bluetooth −250 | 2500, 2250 (as before) | 250, 0 |
  | R 250, Bluetooth 0, USB 400 | 2000 | AirPlay delay 2000; Bluetooth −250, USB +150 | 2000, 1750, 2150 (before: 250, 0, 400, about two seconds ahead of AirPlay) | 250, 0, 400 |
  | R 2500, USB 0 | 2000 | AirPlay delay 2500; USB −2000 (clamped from −2500) | 2500, 500 (before: 2500, 0) | 2000, 0 |
- **S180** *Required.* Windows migration, once. Before this model the Windows app stored per output a trim from 0 to 20 000 ms (`delay_ms` in its settings file, 0 by default) that applied with or without AirPlay, as L_s + min(trim, 3000) while an AirPlay speaker was chosen (L_s as in S178), and per AirPlay speaker its own delay d_s from 0 to 3000 ms (P99). When it loads a settings file of an older format version, the first time it has both that file and an output list, it takes R from S174 and t_R, the reference's stored trim (0 when it has none or there is no reference), and stores:
  - for every other output x with a stored trim t_x: o(x) = clamp(t_x − t_R, −2000, 20 000);
  - the native sender's adjustment A (S178) = t_R, so the reference keeps sounding t_R after L_s;
  - nothing for R; every AirPlay speaker's own delay d_s stays as it is.

  It then writes the file in the new format version (an older build keeps a newer file aside, as it does today). Without AirPlay the outputs keep their alignment (S176 subtracts the smallest offset taking part, so only a delay common to all of them goes). With AirPlay every local output plays exactly as before, h = L_s + t_R + t_x − t_R = L_s + t_x, as long as its old trim was at most 3000 ms (the old cap) and its offset needed no clamping, and the AirPlay speakers keep L_s + d_s, so a user who lined a late AirPlay speaker up by giving R a trim keeps that alignment, now in the AirPlay delay. Where t_R is 0, the default, every offset equals the old trim and A is 0. Example: R 250, Bluetooth 0, TV 120 and an AirPlay speaker with d_s 0 become Bluetooth −250, TV −130 and A = 250; without AirPlay, all on, the hold-backs stay 250, 0 and 120; with AirPlay and L_s = 1750, L = 2000 and they are 2000, 1750 and 1870 with the AirPlay speaker at L_s + 0 = 1750, all as before, and the window shows an AirPlay delay of 2000 (S181).
- **S181** *Required.* What the windows show (UI facts; the wording goes through `UIStrings` on the Mac and the Windows app's own strings):
  - The reference's row has no delay field, whether the reference is on or off.
  - Every other local output's row shows its offset with its sign: "+150 ms", "−250 ms", and "0 ms" for zero. The field takes whole ms with an optional leading "+" or "−" (a hyphen-minus counts as "−"), clamped to S175's range; other input is ignored and the field shows the stored value again.
  - One short hint beside the delay fields says what they are relative to, naming the reference (the built-in speakers on the Mac, the reference's name on Windows), and which way to move a value for a speaker heard first or late; the Mac adds that a negative value is for a speaker that sounds late, such as Bluetooth. With no reference, it says that the speaker with the lowest value plays first and the others wait by the difference.
  - The AirPlay delay (S178) is one field in the AirPlay part of the window, not on any local row, with a hint that the local speakers wait that long for the AirPlay speakers. With the native sender, the only one since 2026-10-20 (with the bundled engine it showed and edited the stored value), it shows L = clamp(L_s + A, 0, 20 000) in whole ms and follows L_s when that changes; entering v stores A = clamp(v − L_s, −2000, 20 000) with the L_s of that moment, so the field then shows v (unless clamped). An AirPlay speaker's own delay d_s (P99), where a window offers it (the Windows app doesn't today), stays a plain 0 to 20 000 ms value.
  - Hold-backs (S176) are not shown.

### Microphone calibration of one output's offset

S232 to S234 let the host measure one local output against the reference with a microphone and fill in that output's offset (S175), instead of the user finding it by ear. The core makes the test signal and does the estimate; the host plays the signal, records, and stores the result. AudioSync's own design from DSP textbook methods (a matched filter, that is a cross-correlation with the known signal); no other project's calibration was studied.

- **S232** *Required.* The measurement. The host plays the test signal (S233) on the reference alone and records it with the microphone, then plays the same signal on the chosen output alone with the microphone where it was, and passes both recordings to the estimate: mono, at the signal's rate, the two outputs at the hold-backs they have while both take part (S176), so the only things that differ between the two runs are the output and its offset. Frame 0 of each recording is the moment the signal enters that output's path, before its hold-back, so the hold-back is part of the arrival; the host trims each recording to that moment, or starts it at the same distance before it in both runs. A gap from recording start to play start that differs between the runs goes into d unseen and can flip the result's sign. Each recording lasts at least the largest hold-back taking part (about 2 s while an AirPlay speaker is chosen, S178, up to 20 s with large offsets), plus the output's latency, plus the 0.75 s signal, plus a margin of about half a second. The host records at 48 kHz: S234's limit of 2^21 frames is about 44 s there but only about 10.9 s at 192 kHz, which a 20 s hold-back would pass. The Mac host records at the input device's own rate and refuses before anything plays, as too long, when the recording would pass that limit there, so a long hold-back at a high microphone rate is never cut short. The estimate finds the signal's arrival in each recording (S234), a_R in the reference's and a_x in the chosen output's, in ms. Only the difference d = a_x − a_R is used, so the microphone's input latency and the time between the signal's start and the recording's start cancel out as long as they are the same in both runs. The new offset is `o_new = clamp(round(o_old − d), −2000, +20 000)` ms, whole ms in S175's range, where o_old is the output's stored offset read as S175 reads it (0 when it isn't finite, else clamped). A result the clamp changed is still accepted but says it was clamped, so the host can tell the user the output stays off by the part cut away. The sign follows S172: a chosen output heard d ms later than the reference sounds late, so it is held back d ms less, and one heard earlier is held back more. A difference larger in size than the whole offset range, 22 000 ms, which no two offsets in range explain, is refused as implausible. A refused measurement, for this reason or one of S234's, never changes the offset. Core tests "calibrate: ...". The signal needs no music: on the Mac a process tap whose source plays nothing delivers no block, so from the moment a calibration fades the music out until it gives the outputs back, the host writes silence in its place, at the capture rate, whenever the capture has given nothing for 40 ms, and the signal enters every path on that silence (logic tests "calibration fill: ..."); the Windows capture's gap fill keeps its ring moving the same way at all times.
- **S233** *Required.* The test signal: a linear sweep from 300 Hz to 8 kHz over 0.75 s, peaking at −18 dBFS (a linear amplitude of 0.125), mono, with a raised-cosine fade of 20 ms at each end so neither end clicks (the first and last millisecond stay under 1 % of the peak). It is defined in continuous time and sampled at the rate asked for, so it is the same sound at any rate: the host may play it at the output's rate and give the estimate the signal made at the microphone's rate. Rates from 22 050 to 384 000 Hz are made (below 22 050 Hz the sweep's top would pass the Nyquist frequency); its length is `round(0.75 × rate)` frames. Why a sweep: its autocorrelation has one sharp peak (the main lobe is about 1 / 7.7 kHz, some 0.13 ms, wide, and outside 3 ms its sidelobes stay near 1 % of the peak), its energy is spread evenly over a band that small speakers and built-in microphones cover, and unlike a noise burst it is the same at every rate. The estimate needs far less than −18 dBFS at the microphone (S234), so the level is chosen to be quiet rather than for the estimate.
- **S234** *Required.* Arrival and confidence. The arrival in a recording is the lag of the largest magnitude of the cross-correlation of the recording with the signal, over every lag where the whole signal lies inside the recording. The correlation runs through a radix-2 FFT of the recording's length rounded up to a power of two, which never wraps those lags: for two 3 s recordings at 48 kHz the whole estimate takes about 45 ms in a release build on the PC, where the plain sum would take seconds. A recording is refused, naming which one, when:
  - *Too short:* it is shorter than the signal plus 100 ms (fewer lags would not show a noise floor), or the signal passed is not the one for the rate.
  - *Too long:* it holds more than 2^21 frames (about 44 s at 48 kHz), which bounds the estimate's memory: at that length it peaks near 96 MB (two 2^21-point complex buffers of 32 MB each, their twiddles and the correlation).
  - *Clipped:* 3 samples in a row have a magnitude of 0.99 or more (the flat top of a clipped waveform; a single full-scale sample, a click, is let through), or a sample isn't finite.
  - *Too quiet:* the correlation is 0 everywhere (silence), or its peak is less than 12 times the median of its magnitude over all lags. Noise alone reaches 6.4 to 7.8 in the core's runs, so it is refused with room to spare; white noise 20 dB louder than the signal (per-sample RMS) still gives about 29; by the matched filter's gain, the square root of the signal's 36 000 frames at 48 kHz (about 190), the threshold lies near −27 dB.
  - *Ambiguous echo:* another lag more than 3 ms from the peak reaches over 0.7 of it. Then a reflection or a second sound is nearly as strong as the arrival and either may be the direct one. Within 3 ms, about 1 m of extra path, a reflection is taken as part of the arrival itself. The signal's own sidelobes outside 3 ms stay near 0.01, and noise alone mostly reaches 0.8 to 0.99, so the check also backs up the one above. A reflection stronger than a blocked direct path is measured as the arrival and is not refused; the host asks for a clear line from the speaker to the microphone.

  The thresholds are constants of the core (`core/src/calibrate.rs`), checked by tests that put noise alone, a signal under noise, and echoes of known strength through them.

### Calibrate All Speakers

S235 to S241 measure every speaker that is on in one run, local outputs, Cast speakers and AirPlay speakers alike, each against the reference as S232 to S234 measure one output, and show the results on a sheet before anything changes. The core plans the run, works out how long a Cast speaker takes, judges each speaker's two sweeps, sets the shared AirPlay delay and lists what Apply writes (`core/src/calibrate_all.rs`); the host plays, records, shows the sheet and stores the changes. AudioSync's own design; no other project's calibration was studied.

- **S235** *Required.* The run. The host names the reference, a local output, and every speaker that is on (for Calibrate Zone, S255, only the zone's present members, S246, that are on, plus the reference), each with its class (local output, Cast speaker or AirPlay speaker), its stored delay or none, and its expected arrival: when its sweep should appear in a recording whose frame 0 is the moment the sweep enters its path before its hold-back (S232), so the hold-back plus the latency as the host estimates it, the reference's included. The host takes that estimate per class from what the engine applies to the speaker's path: for a local output its hold-back plus 0.5 s; for a Cast speaker its wait (S241); for an AirPlay speaker the latency L_s the sender holds the session to, read at run start, or 3000 ms, the AirPlay path's upper bound, while the sender reports none, the AirPlay delay the field shows counting instead when it is longer, plus the hold-back the sender adds for it, plus its reported latency R_s (S283, 0 when it reported nothing), and 0.5 s (`airplay_wait_ms`; a field below L_s moves only the local outputs, so it never shortens the window). The run plays the reference's sweep first, then each other speaker's two sweeps, one speaker at a time with the others silent, in the order given. Each recording lasts the expected arrival plus the 0.75 s sweep plus 1 s, at least the sweep plus 100 ms and at most 2^21 frames (S234); an expected arrival that isn't a finite number counts as 0. A Cast speaker without a stored offset has no estimate: its first window is the 2^21-frame limit, marked so the host can say it is searching, and its second is the first sweep's arrival plus the sweep plus 1 s. A run needs a supported rate (S233), a reference among the speakers, unique IDs, and at least one speaker besides the reference; a run in which any window but a searching Cast speaker's would pass the 2^21-frame limit at that rate is refused as too long before anything plays, since a window cut short loses the sweep and reads as too quiet (at 192 kHz that is an expected arrival past about 9.2 s). Microphone calibration measures external speakers only: the Mac host leaves out of a run, and of the single-speaker calibration, each local output the microphone can't hear, namely one with a virtual transport; a Bluetooth output whose name says headphones; and any other output whose output data source is the headphone jack or whose first output stream's terminal is headphones (or the USB headphones, head-mounted or headset terminal). Over Bluetooth only the name decides: Core Audio documents no kind for Bluetooth outputs, and macOS reports Bluetooth speakers with the headphones terminal as well, so a Bluetooth speaker takes part whatever its terminal or data source. The sheet lists them with the reason and never sweeps them; Include Anyway puts one in the runs of that sheet, for a speaker that reports the wrong kind, until the sheet closes. With the reference on the headphone jack nothing can start (logic tests "calibration: ..." and "calibrate all: ...").
- **S236** *Required.* Two sweeps and the uncertainty. Each sweep's arrival is found as S234 finds it, and its difference from the reference's arrival is how much later the speaker was heard. A difference beyond the 22 000 ms any two offsets in range can span is refused as implausible (S232). A row's difference is the mean of its two sweeps, and its uncertainty U is half their difference plus the 3 ms guard of S234; a row with U over 10 ms is uncertain, shown but never applied. Any refused sweep refuses the row with that reason (too quiet, which includes a peak under 12 times the noise floor, clipped, an ambiguous echo, too short, too long, implausible) and is never averaged with the other; a refused first sweep skips the second, and a refused reference sweep refuses every row and ends the run. The new value, rounded to whole ms: a local output's offset (S175) moves down by the difference, clamped to −2000 to +20 000 ms; a Cast speaker's measured offset and the AirPlay delay (S178) move up by it, since the other outputs wait for those speakers, clamped to 0 to 20 000 ms, the largest hold-back (S176). The Cast sender stores offsets up to 120 000 ms, but a stored Cast offset above 20 000 ms is measured from 20 000 ms, the largest value in use, so one stored at 30 000 ms and heard 40 ms before the reference gets 19 960 ms. A row says it was clamped only when the range cut its value; a value that lands exactly on an end of the range is not clamped.
- **S237** *Required.* The AirPlay delay. AirPlay speakers share one delay (S178), so every AirPlay speaker is measured and the new delay is the old one plus the median of the certain AirPlay rows' differences (the mean of the middle two for an even count). The spread, the largest minus the smallest of those differences, is reported; over 10 ms the sheet says that the AirPlay speakers differ by that much and that only one AirPlay delay can be set, and the median still applies. A new delay the 0 to 20 000 ms range cut says it was clamped, as a row does (S236). Without a certain AirPlay row the delay stays. Reason: AirPlay speakers keep in step among themselves, so a large spread points at the room or the microphone's position rather than at the delay, and averaging it silently would hide that.
- **S238** *Required.* The sheet, Apply and Undo. Nothing changes during the run. The sheet lists every speaker in the order given: the reference, the old value, the new value ± U, or the reason it was refused or not measured. Apply lists one change per certain local or Cast row and one for the AirPlay delay when an AirPlay row is certain, each with the value it replaces (none when the speaker had no stored value); it is refused while no row is certain. One Apply is one change with one Undo, which restores exactly those replaced values. For the AirPlay delay, the value Apply writes and Undo restores is the stored setting of S178, not the latency L it yields, so an L_s that moves after Apply neither counts as a change to it nor shifts what Undo puts back. Undo is withdrawn as soon as a value Apply wrote changes (a local or Cast delay, or the AirPlay setting of S178, more than half a millisecond away from what Apply wrote), so it never overwrites a later change; a new L_s alone keeps it. On the Mac a value the store no longer holds withdraws Undo too, since Undo could not write it back; on Windows a speaker that leaves the list keeps its stored value and does not withdraw Undo. An applied sheet is spent: a second Apply and Measure Again are refused, since its rows describe offsets that are no longer stored and measuring or applying again would undo the correction.
- **S239** *Required.* Measure Again. Any row but the reference's can be measured again: a new run of the reference's sweep and that speaker's two, from the values the sheet started with, whose steps name the sheet's rows. Its result replaces that row alone, and the AirPlay delay is worked out again. A Measure Again run that is cancelled or ended by a change (S240) is dropped: the sheet and that row stay exactly as they were.
- **S240** *Required.* Ending a run. A cancel, a change of the speaker list, a speaker taking part turned off, a switch of the source, any zone click (S245), even one that switches nothing, and, for Calibrate Zone (S255), the measured zone losing a member or being deleted stop the run: no more steps, and its sheet ends with that reason, keeping the rows measured so far for display, with nothing to apply. A zone click and a change to the measured zone end it as a change of the speaker list. A run finished with steps still waiting ends as cancelled.
- **S241** *Required.* A Cast speaker's wait. The expected arrival the host gives a Cast speaker (S235), which is also how long the music already sent to it plays out before the first sweep, comes from one rule both hosts call: its stored offset, counted up to 20 000 ms, plus the Cast feed's hold-back plus 500 ms; with no stored offset (none, or 0 ms, which the sender keeps after Undo) the hold-back plus a starting guess by the format the session plays, 3000 ms for WAV and 6000 ms for FLAC or a format not reported yet (CAST_SPEC C77a; the FLAC figure is derived, not measured). A negative offset, or one that is not a number, also counts as no stored offset, here and for the searching window of S235. The guess never delays another output. Reason: a guess too short lets music overlap the first sweep, one too long only costs time, and leaving the hold-back out made a host wait too little while AirPlay holds the Cast feed back.
- **S255** *Required* (`core/src/calibrate_all.rs`, `zone_plan`, `Run::listing`, `Sheet::measure_again_off`, `airplay_moves`; `asc_calibration_run_create_listing`, `asc_calibration_sheet_measure_again_off`, `asc_calibration_zone_plan`; `CalibrateAllFlow.swift`, `CalibrateAllFlow.zonePlan`, `CalibrateAllFlow.airPlayMoves`, `CalibrateAllFlow.offersZoneRun`, `CalibrateAllFlow.startFailure`, `CalibrateAllFlow.deletingZone`; `AppDelegate+CalibrateAll.swift`, `calibrateZone`; `desktop/src/audio/calibrate_all.rs`, `ZoneScope`; `desktop/src/adapter.rs`, `start_calibrate_zone`; `desktop/src/ui_model.rs`, `AppState::offers_zone_run`). *Calibrate Zone.* "Calibrate Zone…" in a zone's Options menu runs Calibrate All (S235 to S241) over that zone. The item is off while none of the zone's members is present or a calibration holds the outputs, since a start would then do nothing. The sheet's rows are the reference first, then the zone's members in zone order, and the sheet says "Bring the computer into <zone>", since the microphone and the reference stay on the computer. Hidden and absent members and members that play as part of a target (S246, S247) are left out. A present member that is off is listed with "Off" as its reason and never swept, and the run never switches it on; once the user turns it on, Measure Again (S239) measures it from its stored delay and expected arrival as the host reads them then, since the sheet read them while it didn't play. Speakers on outside the zone are silenced in the mix send for the run, never through device volume (S192) or by switching them off, are never measured, and keep their stored values: they carry the faded music, a local output is at gain 0 and an AirPlay speaker muted in its stream while a sweep is on its path, and the run waits until they would have played a sweep out before it lets them be heard. They are restored on every way a run ends (cancel, each S240 end, Apply, Undo), nothing is stored, and a quit mid-run leaves no trace. A zone with only the reference measurable is refused as nothing to measure. The shared AirPlay delay is the median of the zone's certain AirPlay rows (S237), an off row counting toward neither the median nor the old delay. Since it moves every AirPlay speaker, the AirPlay line names, in zone order, each other zone with a listed AirPlay member, plus "speakers in no zone" when a listed AirPlay speaker is in none. Apply and Undo follow S238, Measure Again S239. The results' start button runs that zone again, the speakers outside it silenced again, never a whole run. Once the measured zone is deleted, the results read as Calibrate All's and that button runs every speaker. Reason: the microphone hears one room, so measuring a zone that is a room keeps speakers elsewhere out of the recording without turning anything off (core tests `a_zone_run_…`, `the_airplay_line_…`; logic tests "calibrate zone: …"; Windows tests `a_zone_…`, `deleting_the_measured_zone_ends_the_run`, `the_cards_button_after_a_zone_run_runs_that_zone_again`, `calibrate_zone_is_offered_…`, `the_calibrate_all_card_of_a_zone_run_names_the_zone`, `a_zone_run_is_ticked_once_it_starts`).

## 9. FIFO feed (PCM into the AirPlay engine)

Retired with the FIFO feed on 2026-10-20, all but the resampler of S85 and S94; kept as a record (see the note at the top). S82 went earlier that day with the bundled engine.

- **S82** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayEngine.swift:39-40`, `:156-164`, `:232-241`; metadata pipe: `AirPlayEngine.swift:42-50`, `:125`, `:164-178`; `PipeMetadata.swift:167-171`, `:215-238`). The pipe is a POSIX FIFO named `audiosync.pipe` in the `media` folder of the engine's working directory (`~/Library/Application Support/AudioSync/AirPlay`), mode 0600, created before the engine is launched; a regular file in its place is replaced by a FIFO. Beside it, `audiosync.pipe.metadata` (the engine reads `<pipe>.metadata` while it plays the pipe) carries the track info of S168. It is made the same way before every launch, and the host holds a read end and a write end open on it (both non-blocking) until the engine stops or is launched again, so the engine never reads end-of-file from it. It is optional: one the host can't open is removed, and the PCM pipe works without it.
- **S83** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayEngineConfig.swift:41-42`; tests "config: pipe at 48000 Hz", "config: pipe at 16-bit"; `fifotest.swift:64`, two bytes per sample). Wire format: raw PCM, no header, 48 000 Hz, signed 16-bit, 2 channels interleaved L, R. *Required:* little-endian (native on every supported Mac). One frame is 4 bytes; one second is 192 000 bytes.
- **S84** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`fifotest.swift:18-19`, `:60-61`, `:91`; `logictest.swift:203-210`). Interface: create with the pipe path and a queue length in seconds; `setInputRate(Rc)`; `feed(interleaved, frames)` from the capture thread; `start()`; `stop()`; `samplesQueued`, a monotonic 64-bit count of 16-bit samples accepted into the queue (frames × 2). *Now covered by the core.*
- **S85** *Retired in part with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20)*: the resampler it describes still converts for every local output (S62), at ratio 1 when its device runs at the capture rate, and for the AirPlay sender's input; the pipe it fed is a record. *Observed* (core tests `resampled_frame_counts_keep_pace_with_the_input` in `core/src/ffi/render.rs`, 200 blocks of 441 frames at 44 100, 96 000 and 32 000 Hz to 48 kHz within ±2 frames of the exact count, and the resampler's own in `core/src/resample.rs`; the fifo suite's checks "fifo: 48000 / 44100 / 96000 / 32000 Hz → 48 kHz frame count", 95 697 vs 95 697, 95 999 vs 96 000, 95 917 vs 95 917, 95 916 vs 95 917 from 2 s fed in 441-frame blocks, are a record). Input at Rc is resampled to 48 000 Hz with phase carried across blocks: over any run, output frames are within ±2 of `fed × 48000 / Rc`. At Rc = 48 000 the sender's input passes through unchanged. *Required* (AudioSync design; core tests `at_ratio_one_…` in `core/src/resample.rs`): a local output's converter at equal rates runs at ratio 1, so S206's slow-down and S207's trim reach it. A call that begins with no trim under way copies its frames exactly, each output sample the input sample the converter's fixed latency (21 frames, the kernel's half-width) earlier, bit for bit, a non-finite input sample zeroing what was still to play as S125 says; only a call that begins with a trim under way interpolates with the kernel, as at other rates. The choice is made once per call, so a trim's frames all come from the kernel, and the copy goes on at the delay the trim left. *Required:* at least linear interpolation; a band-limited resampler is RECOMMENDED; no allocation (S126).
- **S86** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* ("fifo: NaN/±inf samples do not crash"). Float → int16 conversion never traps. *Required* mapping: NaN → 0; otherwise `round(clamp(x, −1, +1) × 32767)` (ties away from zero), so +∞ → 32767 and −∞ → −32767.
- **S87** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Required; Observed* sizing (`logictest.swift:203`, 4 s). Between `feed` and the writer thread is a lock-free single-producer, single-consumer queue of `seconds × 48 000` frames (default 4 s). When it is full, `feed` drops the frames that don't fit; dropped frames are not counted in `samplesQueued`. `feed` works whether or not the writer runs. *Now covered by the core.*
- **S88** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* ("fifo: stop/start leaves one writer thread — 1 writers", "fifo: stop closes the writer — 0 writers"). `start()` runs exactly one writer thread holding at most one write descriptor; five back-to-back `stop()`/`start()` pairs still leave exactly one. Within 300 ms of `stop()`, the writer thread has exited and closed its descriptor. *Required:* `start()` while started is a no-op; `start()` begins with an empty queue; `stop()` returns only after the writer thread has closed its descriptor and exited, so a `stop()` followed at once by `start()` (every source switch) never has two writers, two descriptors or two consumers of the queue; this wait is bounded by the writer's polling intervals (S89, S93) and stays under 300 ms.
- **S89** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Required.* The writer opens the pipe write-only in non-blocking mode; per POSIX `open(2)`, that fails with `ENXIO` while the FIFO has no reader instead of waiting for one. The open is retried at least every 100 ms; meanwhile the backlog rule (S90) keeps the queue short.
- **S90** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`fifotest.swift:65-68`). Backlog cap: the writer never lets more than 250 ms (12 000 frames, 48 000 bytes) of unwritten audio pile up; when it falls behind it discards the oldest audio beyond that cap, in whole frames only. Everything else is delivered in order, never duplicated. A new open of the pipe goes further and starts at the live edge (S169), and so does the end of a stall, when the open pipe took nothing for longer than the cap (S203).
- **S91** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* ("fifo: audio reaches the reader — 159744 of 161280 bytes"). With a reader draining the pipe, real-time-paced audio fed for 1 s arrives within 300 ms after feeding stops, short by at most the 250 ms cap (48 000 bytes).
- **S92** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* ("fifo: reader closing mid-stream does not kill the process"; `main.swift:42`, `SIGPIPE` ignored process-wide). When the reader goes away (engine crash or restart), writing MUST NOT kill the process. *Required:* the descriptor also sets `F_SETNOSIGPIPE`; on `EPIPE` the writer closes it and returns to the open loop (S89), so a restarted engine receives audio again, starting at the live edge (S169), without any action from the host.
- **S93** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Required.* On `EAGAIN` (pipe full) the writer sleeps at most 5 ms and retries; the backlog cap (S90) still applies. The pipe carries whole 4-byte frames and the stream MUST stay frame-aligned: a short write (POSIX allows partial non-blocking writes larger than `PIPE_BUF`) resumes at exactly the next unwritten byte; after `EPIPE` the rest of a partly written frame is abandoned and the next connection starts on a frame boundary. A misaligned stream would turn every AirPlay speaker's audio into full-scale noise. The kernel pipe buffer (64 KiB on macOS) is outside AudioSync's control.
- **S94** *Retired in part with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20)*: a new capture rate still resets the resampler's phase and history, for the local outputs and the AirPlay sender's input; the feed's `setInputRate` is a record. *Required.* `setInputRate` is called by the engine at each session start, before the first `feed`; it resets the resampler phase and history.
- **S95** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Required.* No write to the pipe ever happens on the capture thread; `feed` only converts and enqueues (S126).
- **S160** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`fifotest.swift:69-71`, `:84`; `docs/BENCHMARKS.md`, counter definitions; checks "fifo: counters see one open and the bytes written", "fifo: counters see the vanished reader", and the `Counters(…)` values they print). The feed keeps read-only diagnostic counters, read together as `counters`: `opens` (successful opens of the pipe for writing, S89), `writeErrors` (writes that failed with an error other than `EAGAIN`, such as `EPIPE` when the reader went away, S92), `backlogSkips` and `backlogSkipSamples` (discards by the backlog cap, S90, at an open of the pipe, S169, or by a stall of the open pipe, S203, which counts once however many steps it drops audio in, and the 16-bit samples they dropped), `pipeFullWaits` (waits on `EAGAIN`, S93), `shortWrites` (partial writes, S93) and `bytesWritten` (bytes the pipe accepted). After one second with a reader that was there from the start: one open, no write error, and `bytesWritten` at least what the reader received (145 408 bytes for both in one run). After five `stop()`/`start()` pairs, another second and the reader closing: `writeErrors` 1 (at least 1 is asserted), `opens` 2 although the writer keeps retrying the open while no reader exists (S89), and `bytesWritten` 290 816 in the same run, more than the 192 000 bytes that one second of audio makes at 48 kHz (S83); the test feeds a second second after the reader closed, which nothing can write, so the extra bytes come from before the last `start()`. So failed opens count as neither opens nor write errors, and `stop()` and `start()` reset nothing. *Required:* each counter is a monotonic 64-bit count over the feed's life, incremented only by the writer thread with relaxed atomic adds and readable from any thread without blocking it; a snapshot need not be consistent across counters; `backlogSkipSamples` counts two samples per frame, like `samplesQueued` (S84).
- **S169** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (FIFO check "EC7 engine restart: the reopened pipe starts as close to live as a fresh start", which printed "first frame 20 ms behind live after a fresh start, 20 ms after the reopen"; `docs/EDGE_CASES.md` EC7; `CHANGELOG.md` Unreleased, Fixed: after an engine restart AirPlay comes back with the latency it had before, where each restart could leave it up to a quarter of a second later; parity check "rust: fifo reopen resumes at the live edge (S169)", a known gap on the core until its writer followed this clause: it printed 10 ms after a fresh start and 200 ms after the reopen; now a plain check). *Live edge on every open.* Each time the writer opens the pipe (S89), whether it is the first open after `start()` or a reopen after the reader went away (S92, an engine crash or restart, S143), it starts at the live edge, the newest audio fed so far. The audio that queued while nobody read the pipe, up to the 250 ms that S90 lets pile up, is dropped, never written late, and the drop counts as a backlog skip (S160). The first frame written after a reopen is therefore about as close to live as the first frame after a fresh start, however long the reader was gone (20 ms for both in one run); allowing for timing noise, no more than 50 ms further behind live than a fresh start, far below the backlog cap of S90. Without this rule every reopen would deliver the capped backlog first, so the AirPlay leg would come back up to 250 ms later after each engine restart and fall out of step with the local outputs at their saved delays (S80); with it, AirPlay comes back with the latency it had before. While a reader stays connected, S90 governs a writer that falls behind, and S203 a pipe that takes nothing for longer than the cap. *Required:* at each successful open, before its first write, the writer discards every frame queued at that moment; one or more frames dropped this way count as one backlog skip, with two samples per frame in `backlogSkipSamples` (S160); the audio fed after the open is delivered in order and never duplicated (S90), and a frame left partly written when the reader went away is not resumed (S93).
- **S203** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Required* (`docs/EDGE_CASES.md` EC3; maintainer's ear test of 2026-10-18: an AirPlay TV and the Mac's speakers were in step at a 500 ms delay before a sleep, and after the wake the delay still read 500 ms but AirPlay was far behind; core test "after_a_stall_the_open_pipe_resumes_as_close_to_live_as_a_fresh_start", which failed with "first frame 250 ms behind live after the stall, 10 ms after a fresh start" before the core's writer followed this clause; FIFO check "fifo: resumes at the live edge after a stall (S203)"). *Live edge after a stall.* The pipe *stalls* when its reader keeps it open but stops reading (the engine pauses its player, for instance while its speakers reconnect after a wake), so the pipe fills, writes are refused (`EAGAIN`, or a write that takes nothing, S93) and the unwritten audio grows past the backlog cap: the pipe took nothing for longer than the 250 ms of S90. No reopen follows, so S169 never fires, and S90 alone would leave the newest 250 ms waiting for the reader, which would then play them first: AirPlay would come back up to 250 ms later than before the stall and stay that far out of step with the local outputs at their saved delays (S80). So from the step where a stall begins until the pipe takes data again, the writer keeps only the newest audio, at most one write's worth (128 frames, under 3 ms, S93), and drops everything older: one backlog skip for the whole stall, however long it lasts, with every frame it drops in `backlogSkipSamples` (S160); the first frame the pipe takes after the stall is then as close to live as after an open (S169), no more than 50 ms further behind live than after a fresh start (in the core test: 2.7 ms after the stall, 10 ms after a fresh start). Audio dropped: what waited when the stall began, up to the cap's 250 ms, and what is fed during it, except the newest write's worth; the rest of a partly written frame is still completed (S93), and nothing is written twice or out of order (S90). A pipe that takes nothing while less than the cap piles up (a short stall the cap absorbs) loses nothing: the audio that waited follows on without a gap. A backlog past the cap while the pipe keeps taking data (a writer that wasn't scheduled for a while) is not a stall: S90 alone governs it. The audio already in the kernel's pipe buffer when the writes began to fail (up to 64 KiB, about a third of a second, on macOS, S93) is beyond the writer's reach and reaches the reader first. *Required* detail: a stall begins at a step that finds the last write refused and more than `BACKLOG_CAP_FRAMES` frames unwritten (counting a partly written frame as one), and ends once the pipe has taken a whole write's worth (512 bytes) since it began, or at a disconnect (S92) or an open (S169). A pipe its reader left nearly full can still take a few shorter writes, less than 512 bytes in all, and they must not end the stall: the backlog would grow back towards the cap before the next refusal began a new one. A write interrupted by a signal changes nothing. The writer thread does this with no allocation (S129), and the capture side is unchanged (S95, S126). *Unverified risk* (review of 2026-10-19): an engine that kept the pipe full during normal playback, refusing writes now and then, would let clock drift push the backlog slowly past the cap; S90 alone trimmed a few frames there, and this rule drops about 250 ms at once. The 60-minute soak (`Tests/soak.sh`) reports the backlog skip counter, which should stay flat during steady playback; one that climbs points here.

## 10. AirPlay engine coupling

Retired with the bundled engine on 2026-10-20, all but S111 and part of S101 and S106; kept as a record (see the note at the top).

- **S96** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayEngineConfig.swift:13-52`; tests "config: …"). The engine runs only as a separate process, configured through a file regenerated on every start: API bound to 127.0.0.1, no trusted networks, `admin_password` = this run's random password, no allowed browser origin, websocket port = API port − 1, `start_buffer_ms = 500`, library password = the same password, library port = API port (3689 in the tests), library directory = `<dir>/media` (holds the pipe), `pipe_autostart = true`, `pipe_sample_rate = 48000`, `pipe_bits_per_sample = 16`, local audio output disabled, MPD port 0, log level `log` (or `debug` when diagnostics are on). The config's `uid` setting names the user the engine runs as, the one running AudioSync (test "config: runs as the given user").
- **S97** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayEngine.swift:183-217`; `AirPlayEngineConfig.swift:7-8`, `:56-58`; tests "config: no password left after start", "config: both passwords marked removed", "config: nothing else changes when the password is removed"). The config file is mode 0600. The host polls `GET /api/config` with the password every 0.5 s, up to 120 times; once it gets HTTP 200, it rewrites the file with every occurrence of the password replaced by `removed-after-start` and nothing else changed.
- **S98** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`EngineLifecycle.swift:4-109`; `AirPlayEngine.swift:297-308`; tests "engine: …"). Engine process states: `stopped`, `starting`, `running` (API accepted this run's password), `restarting`, `failed(setup | launch | unsupportedOS(minimum))`, `gaveUp`. Rules: at most 5 starts within any 60 s window; the 6th is refused and the state becomes `gaveUp`. An unexpected exit restarts the engine after 2 s, unless it happened less than 3 s after the start, before the API answered, on a macOS major version below the bundled engine's minimum: then `failed(unsupportedOS)` and no restart. An unknown minimum never counts as unsupported. An API answer only moves `starting` to `running` (a stale answer during `restarting` is ignored); no answer in time → `failed(launch)`; setup failure → `failed(setup)`. `failed` and `gaveUp` offer Retry, which clears the start history and returns to `stopped`; a stop returns to `stopped`. Spaced exits (one per 20 s) never give up.
- **S99** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Engine.swift:51-56`, `:90-113`; `AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:35-38`; `AppDelegate+Groups.swift:50-55`, `:125-128`; `AppDelegate+AirPlay.swift:90-105`, `:179-183`; engine client `:176-267`, `:296-298`; engine player `:18-26`, `:29-141`; tests "pipe: …"). Pipe playback. The engine starts the pipe by itself as soon as PCM arrives in it while it isn't playing it (`pipe_autostart`, S96) and reports `pause` until its speakers play; adding the pipe then would start it a second time (S168). It also pauses the pipe while it delivers too little audio and resumes that pause by itself once enough comes again; a pause from elsewhere (a receiver's remote, a failed speaker or volume change) it doesn't resume. So the host requests pipe playback in two steps, and only steps in where audio kept reaching the pipe. It notes the capture's frame count (S17) at the trigger, when step one asks and when step two begins; audio reached the pipe throughout when each count is higher than the one before (a count that stays put is a silent source, such as a paused app, whose tap delivers no frames; one that falls is a new session). Triggers: a session start (S116); while streaming, an AirPlay speaker's own click, on or off (S119), a group click, an auto-connect, or the answer to the switch-on after an accepted code (S107); and polls (below). Step one, 0.5 s after the trigger: no session running → nothing; no AirPlay speaker enabled → `PUT /api/player/stop`; otherwise `GET /api/player` (2 s timeout): `play` → done; `pause`, `stop` or no answer → step two, 2 s after that answer. A player status is an HTTP 200 answer with a JSON object whose `state` is `play`, `pause` or `stop`; anything else counts as no answer. Step two, only if a session still runs with an AirPlay speaker enabled: `GET /api/player` again, through the write queue (S101) after the writes queued before it: `play` or no answer → done; `pause` or `stop` without audio throughout → done (a resume would restart a pipe with nothing to play, and a paused player gets its resume, from the engine or from the request made as audio comes back, below; a stopped one gets its start from the engine as audio arrives, and audio that began during the wait means that start may come any moment, so an add would race it); `pause` → `PUT /api/player/play`; `stop` → search the library for the track whose path includes `audiosync.pipe` (`GET /api/search?type=tracks&expression=path includes "audiosync.pipe"`, percent-encoded), no such track → done; then `GET /api/player` once more and, only if it still answers `stop`, `POST /api/queue/items/add?uris=library:track:<id>&clear=true&playback=start` with the first track's id. Nothing is retried within a request. While streaming with an enabled, listed speaker, a poll the engine answers makes a new request right after an engine restart (S102), when any speaker appears in the list or the poll selects any speaker (S104), when audio came back (the capture's frame count, noted at each such poll, rises after it stood still between the two notes before, within one session; a poll that doesn't stream to an enabled, listed speaker starts the notes over), and otherwise once more than 15 s have passed since the last request made by a session start or a poll. A session stop sends `PUT /api/player/stop` at once.
- **S168** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AppDelegate+AirPlay.swift:283-286`, `:289-337`; `AppDelegate+Windows.swift:209`; engine client `:176-185`, `:269-294`; engine player `:15-40`, `:63-65`, `:143-162`; tests "track info: …"). Track info after every start of the pipe. Each start of the pipe, by the host (S99) or by the engine itself (after a restart, or when audio arrives again), is a new queue item: AirPlay receivers then show the pipe's file name until the track info (title, artist, album, artwork) goes into the metadata pipe (S82) again, and two starts close together can leave that pipe unread. The host sends track info while a session runs with at least one AirPlay speaker enabled and this build bundles the engine, as checked at every model update. Meanwhile it asks `GET /api/player` (2 s timeout) every 2.5 s, skipping a turn while the last question is unanswered; the timer also runs while a menu or an alert is open. A player status (S99) whose player holds a queue item (`play` or `pause` with a non-zero `item_id`) other than the one the track info was last sent for, or any item when none has been seen since sending began, makes the host forget what it sent, send the current track info again from scratch and remember that item with the engine run it asked (S102). An item of another run is another item whatever its `item_id`: the engine's JSON API documents queue item ids without promising anything across runs, so a restarted engine may number its queue as the old one did. A stopped player (whatever `item_id` it names), the same item of the same run or no answer changes nothing. When sending ends, the questions stop and what was sent is forgotten, so the next start of sending sends everything again.
- **S100** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (tests "outputs: …"; engine client `:88-92`). The engine's `/api/outputs` list is parsed into speakers: only types "AirPlay 1" and "AirPlay 2" with both an `id` and a `name`; fields `name`, `type`, `selected`, `volume`, `needs_auth_key` (needs a PIN), `has_password`; missing fields default to not selected, volume 50, no PIN, no password; `requires_auth` alone does not mean a PIN is needed. A field that is `null` or of another type counts as missing, unknown fields are ignored, an element that is `null` or not an object is skipped without losing the others, and a volume outside 0–100 is clamped to that range. Invalid JSON, a wrong shape or a top-level array give an empty list. The Mac's own AirPlay receiver (name equal to the computer name, ignoring case; an unknown computer name hides nothing) is left out.
- **S101** *Retired in part with the bundled engine (2026-10-20)*: the engine API transport (the Observed part below) is a record, and so is the host's 3.0 s poll, which events and a 30 s safety net replaced the same day (the Required part). *Required* (`AppDelegate.swift`, `startSafetyNet` and `scheduleDueReconcile`; `AppLifecycle.nextDueReconcile`; `NativeSenderStarts.restartAt`; `LocalNetworkProbe.recheckAt`; `LocalNetworkHint.dueAt`; tests "reconcile: …", "EC7 native airplay: the restart falls due exactly when a reconcile may start the sender again (S101)", "local network: the host looks again exactly when an empty list brings up the soft hint (S101)", "local network probe: a denied answer is asked again 10 s after the browser started (S101)"). Events drive the host's reconciles. Core Audio's device-list notice (bursts merged over 0.3 s) and a lost output (S68) list the local devices again and attach the speakers that are on (S161). AudioSync's own AirPlay sender, which the host asks for discovery changes and speaker events every 250 ms while it runs, updates the AirPlay rows, and the sender's speakers follow at once; so they do after every switch, group click, auto-connect, start and stop of streaming. The launch, Refresh Devices and Retry run the AirPlay reconcile, which starts the sender when none runs; a wake (S139) runs it and then lists the local devices again, since devices may have come or gone during the sleep. Three moments have no event, so the host runs the AirPlay reconcile, then updates the model, at the earliest of them: a restart of the sender `NativeSenderStarts.retrySpacing` (10 s) after a failed start, the recheck of a denied Local Network answer 10 s after its browser started (`LocalNetworkProbe.deniedRecheck`), and the moment a list that stays empty brings up the soft Local Network hint (S154); never while the Mac sleeps, and never sooner than 1 s after the last such reconcile. As a safety net, every 30 s the host runs the AirPlay reconcile and then lists the local devices, for what no event reports: a device that changes in place (its name, its output channels, or its alive flag coming back without a change to the device list) and a notice that never came. Both timers run in the default run loop mode, so they wait while a menu or a modal alert (a PIN prompt) is open. *Observed* (`AppDelegate.swift:300-307`; engine client `:19-27`, `:97`). The host polls every 3.0 s: the engine's output list (2 s timeout, at most one poll in flight, none while the Mac sleeps, S139), then the local device list. Engine API writes go through one serial queue with a 3 s default request timeout (10 s for a switch-on that waits for its result, 15 s for pairing, engine client `:124`, `:140`); output IDs are percent-encoded in request paths (unreserved characters only, `AirPlayReconcile.swift:510-516`; test "outputs: an output id is one percent-encoded path segment").
- **S102** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayReconcile.swift:93-118`, `:226-228`; tests "reconcile: poll timeout during an in-flight switch-on is not an outage", "… a failed poll with nothing in flight is an outage", "… a busy write doesn't make a down engine reachable"). Reachability after a poll = `polled || (writesBusy && previously)`. A newly reachable engine after an unreachable period counts as "came back" (a restart). So does an answer from a different engine run, even when no poll failed in between (each engine start is a new run with its own per-run password, and a poll records the run it was sent to): a crash restart can complete between two polls (tests "EC7 engine restart: a restart with no failed poll still re-selects the speaker", "… a restart during a switch-on still counts as a restart"). Further answers from the same run are not another restart ("… the next answer from the same engine is not another restart").
- **S103** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayReconcile.swift:27-87`; `AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:247-279`; tests "reconcile: …", "EC3 wake: a switch-on that fails after wake is tried again at the next poll, not at once"). Switch-on tracking: each `selected: true` request increments an in-flight count per speaker; only the answer to the last request in flight decides. A success opens a 3 s settle window: an "off" report is ignored while a request is in flight, if it was requested before the success, or within 3 s after it. A failure leaves no grace: while streaming, with the speaker still wanted, the host polls the engine again at once and that report decides (S104, S106). During the wake grace (S139), a report requested before the failure or less than 0.5 s after it (which covers the poll the failure asks for) is too soon to switch the speaker on again; the next regular poll is not. A new request clears both the success and the failure.
- **S104** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayReconcile.swift:257-307`; tests "reconcile: …", "EC3 wake: …"). Per reported speaker, while streaming: engine state matches the wish → keep (and confirm a pending pairing, S107); not wanted but selected → deselect; wanted but off → wait if its switch-on is in flight (even after a restart); else select again if the engine came back or the speaker just appeared; else wait while settling; else, during the wake grace (S139), an `open` speaker (S106) is selected again, or waits when the report is too soon after its last failed switch-on (S103), so it is tried at most once per poll; else the pairing rules (S106–S107) decide wait, ask or drop. When not streaming, nothing is selected or dropped. Selections are applied before drops.
- **S105** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AppDelegate+AirPlay.swift:63-82`; `AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:58-98`, `:239-279`; `AirPlayReconcile.swift:120-139`, `:312-536`; engine client `:115-131`; tests "EC7 engine restart: …", "volume: …"). AirPlay volumes. The host keeps each AirPlay speaker's own volume (S73) and sends the engine per-speaker volumes only, each `v = round(own × master / 100)`; a speaker without an own volume uses 60. Every switch-on sends `{"volume": v}`, then `{"selected": true, "volume": v}` (`PUT /api/outputs/{id}`), so the speaker starts at `v` rather than at the volume the engine holds for it (its database's, stale after a kill, or the engine's master volume, often 100, for a speaker it never saved); only `AirPlayVolume` builds these bodies, and the engine client's `select` sends both, in order (the suites fail otherwise). A start switches every enabled, listed speaker on that way (S116). A start and a poll send their requests volumes first, then switch-offs, then switch-ons: a switch-on holds the engine's one write queue for up to 10 s (S101), while a speaker the engine already plays by itself keeps its database volume until its own request gets through. When a switch-on answers that the speaker connected and its `v` changed meanwhile (a master or slider move can ride a volume request queued before it, which the switch-on then overwrites), the current `v` is sent. Each poll: a speaker without an own volume takes the reported one, at most 60, and no other report changes a speaker's own volume. While streaming, an enabled speaker the engine reports selected, other than one the poll selects again (S104), is sent `v` in the first answer after an engine restart, in the restore answer and in the answer where it first appears, whatever it reports, since a new engine or a newly found speaker plays at the engine's own idea of its volume; afterwards it is sent `v` again whenever it reports another volume, unless its switch-on is settling (S103) or the host sent it a volume less than 2 s ago. The restore answer is the first answer after a restart, while streaming, that lists an enabled speaker: a new engine can answer before it has found the speakers again. A stop before that drops it, since a start sends every speaker its volume (S116). Pipe playback is ensured at once (S99). For a speaker the host doesn't want on, or any speaker while it doesn't stream (the engine may switch one on by itself from its database after a crash or a SIGTERM), no poll sends a volume, and a report never replaces an own volume it already has.
- **S106** *Retired in part with the bundled engine (2026-10-20)*: only the access kinds remain (`AirPlayPairing.Access`, which the AirPlay sender's receivers map to, `NativeReceiver.access`); the decisions on the engine's reports are a record. *Observed* (`AirPlayPairing.swift:19-45`, `:70-95`; `AppDelegate+AirPlay.swift:107-110`; tests "pairing: …", "availability: …"). Access kinds: `open`; `code(password)` when a PIN is needed (`password` when the speaker also has a password); `legacyPassword` for an AirPlay 1 speaker with a password and no PIN flag. Availability shown: open → open; code → "needs code"; legacyPassword → "password unsupported". A wanted speaker the engine reports off, with no pairing under way: `open` → drop as unreachable; `legacyPassword` → drop with its own reason; `code` → ask (PIN or password prompt) only if the user turned it on themselves within 45 s (`promptWindow`) before the report was requested, otherwise drop as "needs code". A report requested before the user's click → wait. One click answers one refusal only.
- **S107** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayPairing.swift:48-128`; tests "pairing: …"). Pairing stages per speaker: asking → (code entered; a second code is ignored) pairing → (speaker re-selected at time t) confirming(since t). While asking or pairing, reports → wait. Confirming: a report requested before t → wait; an "on" report requested at or after t → done; an "off" report at or after t → drop as "still needs code" (code access) or unreachable. A cancel, a turn-off or an automatic turn-on forgets the speaker (a later code or re-select is ignored).
- **S108** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AirPlayPairing.swift:130-152`; tests "pairing queue: …"). Prompts wait in a FIFO queue, at most one per speaker, and only for a speaker currently asking; prompts are shown one at a time. When the engine stops listing a speaker, its pairing stage and queued prompt are dropped, but the time of the user's click is kept, so a speaker that returns within the prompt window still gets its prompt; a speaker that vanished mid-pairing and returns gets a notice, not a second prompt.
- **S109** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`AppDelegate+AirPlay.swift:112-122`). A dropped speaker is removed from the enabled set, gets a notice with its reason (an alert only for a rejected code) and, if nothing else is enabled, streaming stops.
- **S110** *Retired with the bundled engine (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Observed* (`EngineLifecycle.swift:111-126`; `AppDelegate.swift:390-408`; `AirPlayEngine.swift:148-151`; tests "shutdown: …"). Quit: stop the session and deselect every AirPlay speaker; wait for already queued API writes for at most 1.5 s (a stuck write costs only the timeout; an idle queue returns at once); then `SIGTERM` the engine, wait up to 3 s, then `SIGKILL`. Quitting waits at most 5 s on the engine in total (`drainTimeout + gracefulStop ≤ 5`).
- **S111** *Observed* (`main.swift`, signals; `AppLifecycle.exitSignals`, `SignalQuit`; tests "quit: …"). On `SIGTERM`, or `SIGINT` (Ctrl-C in the terminal that launched it), the app marks itself as quitting, so a second launch arriving meanwhile waits for it to go instead of being answered. It then runs the same steps as Quit, on the main queue: the outputs and the AirPlay inputs fade out (S186), then AudioSync's own AirPlay sender tears down, so every speaker gets its TEARDOWN; the Google Cast sender's teardown (about 3.5 s at most) starts first and runs beside them. Once they are done it exits with status 128 plus the signal's number (143 for `SIGTERM`, 130 for `SIGINT`, `AppLifecycle.exitStatus`), so a calling script sees the interrupt. The wait for those steps is bounded at 4 s (`SignalQuit.limit`: the fades' 500 ms cap plus about 2 s for the AirPlay sender's teardown, and the Cast sender's 3.5 s beside them, with room; 3 s before the Cast sender came in): if the main queue doesn't get to them in time or a step hangs, the app exits with the same status anyway, so a signal always ends it. An open modal alert doesn't hold the steps back, since its run loop serves the main queue as well; the bound covers anything else that keeps the main queue busy. A second exit signal while the steps run (a second Ctrl-C) exits at once with its own status. Exactly one exit happens, whichever of these comes first, and the steps never run twice when Quit and a signal meet. Before 2026-10-20 a signal exited at once, with neither the fades nor the sender's teardown.

## 11. Engine control interface and session lifecycle

- **S112** *Observed* (calls in `AppDelegate+Engine.swift`, `AppDelegate+Outputs.swift`, `AppDelegate.swift`). Engine interface: `start(source)` (throws), `stop()`, `isRunning`, `framesCaptured`, `attachOutput(uid, device, delayMs, gain)` (throws), `detachOutput(uid)`, `detachOutputs(uids)`, `activeOutputUIDs`, `setGain(uid, g)`, `setDelay(uid, ms)`, equalizer settings update, and two callbacks: `onOutputGone(uid)` and `interrupted(formatChanged | sourceLost(error))`.
- **S113** *Required; Observed* usage. Every control call is made on the main thread, and both callbacks are delivered on the main thread.
- **S114** *Required.* `start(source)`, called only while stopped (the host stops a running engine first, `AppDelegate+Engine.swift:41`): reset the ring (S39), determine Rc, set the equalizer rate (S30), then start capture (setting the FIFO input rate and starting it, S94 and S88, went with the FIFO feed on 2026-10-20). On any failure it throws and leaves everything stopped and released.
- **S115** *Superseded in part by S186*: before capture stops, every running output and the AirPlay inputs fade out, and the outputs' IO stops only once their fades have played. *Required; Observed* in part (`AppDelegate+Engine.swift:55`, outputs are added again after every start). `stop()` stops capture (no capture callback runs after it returns), stops and removes every renderer, and sets `running` false (it also stopped the FIFO feed, retired on 2026-10-20). It is idempotent.
- **S116** *Retired in part with the bundled engine (2026-10-20)*: ensuring pipe playback (S99) is a record; the AirPlay selection now goes to AudioSync's own sender (`docs/AIRPLAY_SPEC.md`), and the rest stands. *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Engine.swift:16-20`, `:42-57`; `AirPlayReconcile.swift:387-393`, `:474-483`). The host starts a session only with at least one speaker enabled; after a successful start it applies the AirPlay selection (each enabled, listed speaker switched on at its own volume times the master, S105; never the engine's master volume), adds the local outputs, ensures pipe playback (S99) and starts the capture check (S18).
- **S117** *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:197-237`). Outputs are added only while running. Output sync is idempotent: add every enabled, present UID that has no renderer (with its effective delay and gain); remove every renderer whose UID is no longer enabled; an enabled UID that isn't present waits for a later sync.
- **S161** *Observed* (`SpeakerState.swift:67-104`; `AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:197-231`; tests "EC1 unplug: …", "EC1 replug: …", "EC1 switch off: …", "EC2 Bluetooth: …", "EC10 many speakers: eight local speakers are attached together"). The output sync of S117 is a plan computed from three sets of device UIDs, *enabled*, *active* (has a renderer) and *available* (listed now): attach `enabled ∩ available − active` and detach `active − enabled`, each sorted by UID, with no limit on how many. A renderer whose UID is still enabled is never detached by a sync, whether or not its device is listed (a lost device's renderer is gone already, S68), with one exception: the host remembers the device object ID each renderer was started on, and an enabled, running UID listed now under a different object ID (its device came back under a new ID without an "is alive" notice) is restarted: its renderer stops and a new one starts on the listed device with its current delay and gain (tests "EC1 new ID: …"). An enabled, running UID that isn't listed is left alone. An enabled speaker whose device is missing (unplugged, or a Bluetooth headset out of range) stays enabled and is attached by the first sync that finds its device listed again, while the other speakers keep playing.
- **S118** *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:44-49`). When the last speaker (local or AirPlay) is switched off while running, the session stops and every AirPlay speaker is deselected.
- **S119** *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:14-42`). Turning a speaker on while not running starts a session (user origin); while running, a local speaker is synced in and an AirPlay speaker is selected.
- **S162** *Observed* (`SpeakerState.swift:61-99`; `AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:52-84`; `AppDelegate+Engine.swift:28-44`; tests "EC8 all off: …", "EC1 unplug: streaming keeps running while the only speaker is unplugged"). Streaming policy, from the enabled sets and what plays: there is something to stream to while at least one AirPlay or local speaker is enabled, listed or not. A running session is idle, and stops (S118), only when no speaker at all is enabled, so a speaker that is on but unplugged or out of reach keeps it running, and one AirPlay speaker still on is enough. Switching a speaker on starts streaming only while nothing streams (S119); switching one off never starts it, and with nothing streaming there is nothing to stop. A start the user asks for (the power switch, a phone, a preset) with no speaker enabled starts anyway: the speaker list says no speaker is on, the first speaker switched on joins, and such a session is idle only once a speaker has played in it and none is left. A speaker counts once it plays (a local output that started, an AirPlay speaker the sender plays to, a Cast speaker in Playing); one switched on that fails before it plays, such as an AirPlay speaker that asks for a code every time, leaves the session waiting with the no-speaker line. A capture rebuild or a source switch of a session carries on with no speaker enabled only while no speaker has played in it; once the last one went off, an automatic rebuild or switch (the source-quit retry included) stops the session instead. A start AudioSync makes on its own with no speaker enabled starts nothing and asks the user to choose a speaker first (an informational "start" notice, S116, S136). On a first install (nothing saved before the launch) the first built-in output is switched on once, with the first device list that isn't empty, and never again. *Windows design choice*, not observed on the Mac (`desktop/src/adapter.rs`, `enabled_local`; test `a_speaker_on_that_wasnt_listed_since_launch_doesnt_count_as_on`): a local output saved as on that no device listing has shown since launch doesn't count until it is listed: Windows lists a USB device plugged into another USB port under a new endpoint ID, and the old ID would otherwise keep a session running, and the start notice away, with no row the user could switch off.
- **S120** *Observed* (`AppLifecycle.swift:7-16`; `AppDelegate.swift:167-171`; `AppDelegate+Groups.swift:112-133`; `AppDelegate+Windows.swift:129-137`; `WebRemote.swift`, `apply`). Start origins: *user*, *remote* and *automatic* (auto-connect, format-change restart). *Remote* is a phone's command through the web remote (S256 to S258, S266): like *user* it clears the start notices and ends both hold-offs below (the core's gate takes it as the user acting), but a failure it causes only posts a notice and never shows an alert, since nobody may be at the computer. A failed automatic start suppresses further automatic starts until the user acts; switching streaming off by hand suppresses automatic starts by auto-connect (speakers may still be enabled).
- **S121** *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Engine.swift:61-67`; Windows `desktop/src/adapter.rs`, start failure: `gate.start_failed`, then `audio.stop()`). A failed start stops everything and posts a "start" notice (plus an alert for a user start). The AirPlay speakers the user chose stay chosen (`AppDelegate+Engine.swift`, `failStart`). Before 2026-10-21 this clause said every AirPlay speaker was deselected, which the bundled engine's host did and neither app has done since that engine went (2026-10-20).
- **S122** *Observed* (tests "speaker: …"). A speaker that is enabled shows *playing* while streaming and *will play* while not; a disabled one shows *off*. A click on a playing speaker turns it off; a click on a waiting or off speaker turns it on (and starts streaming).

## 12. Non-finite values

- **S123** *Observed* ("eq: recovers from a NaN sample (silence stays silent)", "fifo: NaN/±inf samples do not crash"). NaN and ±infinity can arrive from capture. No stage may trap, crash or abort on them: the enabled equalizer zeroes them (S29); a disabled equalizer passes them into the ring unchanged; the renderer zeroes them at its output (S65); the FIFO conversion maps them (S86).
- **S124** *Superseded in part by S175 and S178*: a local output's offset ranges from −2000 to +20 000 ms, the bundled engine's AirPlay delay from 0 to 20 000 ms and the native sender's adjustment from −2000 to +20 000 ms. *Observed* (`AppDelegate+Outputs.swift:51-106`). Host-side numeric inputs are sanitized before reaching the engine: non-finite delays are ignored, delays are clamped to 0…20 000 ms, volumes to 0…100.
- **S125** *Required.* A non-finite value never persists in any stateful stage: once finite input resumes, every output is finite within one block (the resampler of S85 resets its history if it sees a non-finite sample).

## 13. Real-time constraints

- **S126** *Required* (project rule; the equalizer's design follows it, `Equalizer.swift:7-9`). The audio path is the capture callback (equalizer, ring write, FIFO `feed`) and every renderer callback (reader, conversion, gain). On it, nothing may allocate or free memory, take a lock (mutex, read-write lock, semaphore, dispatch sync, runtime calls that may lock), block or sleep, make a system call, log or build strings, or touch the file system. *Now covered by the core.*
- **S127** *Required.* Work per callback is bounded and linear in the block size: O(N) per reader, O(20·N) biquad steps for the equalizer, O(N) for conversion.
- **S128** *Required.* Values shared with audio threads are lock-free atomics only (an implementation SHOULD enforce this at compile time where its language allows); all buffers (ring, FIFO queue, equalizer state, converter buffers sized for the largest device buffer, at least 8192 frames) are allocated before the audio threads start and freed only after they stopped.
- **S129** *Retired with the FIFO feed (2026-10-20); kept as a record.* *Required.* The FIFO writer thread does not allocate after `start`, shares no lock with the audio path, does no logging inside its loop, and performs only `open`, `write`, `close` and short sleeps.
- **S130** *Observed* (`Equalizer.swift:97-104`, `:128-137`). Control-to-audio updates never make the audio thread wait: scalars are atomic stores; multi-value updates are double-buffered and flipped atomically; the control side MAY spin or yield briefly. *Now covered by the core.*
- **S131** *Required.* Logging about audio-path events (underruns, corrections, drops) happens only from non-real-time threads, from counters the audio path increments atomically. The debug log (`AUDIOSYNC_DEBUG=1`, `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`) is never written from the audio path.

## 14. Threading

- **S132** *Required; Observed* in part (`Equalizer.swift`, `AppDelegate*.swift`). Threads and ownership:

  | Thread | Runs | Writes | Reads |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | main | control interface, callbacks, polls, equalizer settings | reader targets and resync flags, gains, equalizer idle set and active index, FIFO start/stop | counters, `lagFrames`, `framesCaptured` |
  | capture (one Core Audio IO thread) | equalizer, ring write, FIFO `feed` | ring frames and W, equalizer state, FIFO queue tail, `samplesQueued` | equalizer active set, FIFO queue head |
  | one renderer IO thread per output | reader, conversion, gain | its reader's P, F, `lagFrames`, `framesRendered` | ring frames and W, its target, resync flag, gain |
  | FIFO writer (one) | dequeue, backlog cap, pipe writes | FIFO queue head | FIFO queue tail |

- **S133** *Required.* Atomics and orderings: W release/acquire, plus the writer's release fence after publishing W and the reader's acquire fence before re-loading it (S37, S40); reader target delay, gain and FIFO run flag relaxed (a target is stored before its resync flag, and the flag is set with release and taken with an acquire exchange); `framesRendered`, `lagFrames` and `samplesQueued` relaxed; FIFO queue indices release/acquire; equalizer `active` and `reading` indices sequentially consistent (S31). `framesCaptured` is W.
- **S134** *Required.* Teardown order: stop a device's IO (Core Audio returns after the callback in progress finished) before freeing what that callback uses; stop capture before resetting the ring; stop the FIFO writer before freeing its queue.
- **S135** *Required.* Ring samples are stored as relaxed atomic 32-bit words holding the float bit patterns (e.g. `AtomicU32` in Rust), so a reader racing the writer's overwrite is not undefined behavior; on mainstream targets these compile to plain loads and stores. Ordering comes from the W release/acquire pair, the writer's release fence (S37), the reader's acquire fence and the post-copy check (S40). No locks.

## 15. Error states

- **S136** *Observed* (sources cited per row); host reactions are listed for completeness. *Retired in part with the bundled engine and the FIFO feed (2026-10-20)*: the rows for the pipe and the AirPlay engine are a record, and an AirPlay speaker is now dropped when AudioSync's own sender reports that it can't play it (`docs/AIRPLAY_SPEC.md`), with a notice that gives the reason, an alert only for a rejected code, and a stop when no other speaker is enabled.

  | Condition | Engine signal | Host reaction |
  |---|---|---|
  | no speaker enabled at an automatic start | none (host checks) | informational "start" notice (`AppDelegate+Engine.swift`, `startEngine`); a start the user asked for goes on (S162) |
  | microphone denied for an input source | none (host checks) | start fails, notice (`AppDelegate+Engine.swift:21-40`) |
  | capture can't start | `start` throws | stop (the chosen AirPlay speakers stay chosen), "start" notice, alert if user (S121) |
  | audio never arrives | no captured frame carries sound | waiting / not-working notice (S18) |
  | capture format change | `interrupted(formatChanged)` | automatic restart (S20) |
  | capture lost | `interrupted(sourceLost)` | stop (the chosen AirPlay speakers stay chosen), "source" notice (S21) |
