A volume already saved under the new ID wins over the old one; a group that already lists the new key drops the old one and otherwise keeps its order; a set never holds an ID twice. Every other ID and key stays as it is: local keys, IDs of receivers discovery hasn't listed, receivers without a device ID, anything not in the engine's form. An ID therefore moves once, when its receiver is first seen, and a second run changes nothing. The old ID is gone afterwards: back on the bundled engine (the hidden option), a moved speaker is off and outside its groups, like any speaker that engine never saw. The debug log has one line per moved ID. *Unverified* (no receiver here): that the bundled engine's output ID is the device ID in decimal; if it isn't, nothing matches, nothing moves, and the saved speakers stay off under the native sender, as before this clause. To check on a Mac with a receiver: a group saved under the bundled engine keeps its AirPlay speaker after the switch.
- **S151** *Observed* (tests "devices: …"). The local output list hides virtual, aggregate and auto-aggregate devices and macOS's own AirPlay output by default; a hidden device that is chosen (enabled, auto-connect, in a group, or already listed) stays listed in Core Audio order; "Show Virtual Devices" lists everything; hardware transports (built-in, USB, Bluetooth, HDMI, …) and unknown transports are never hidden.
- **S152** *Observed* (tests "layout: …"). Transport types map to tags (built-in, Bluetooth and Bluetooth LE → Bluetooth, USB, HDMI, DisplayPort, Thunderbolt, macOS AirPlay, FireWire, virtual, aggregate and auto-aggregate → aggregate, PCI, AVB; unknown → other); only built-in outputs are "This Mac", in Core Audio order; every other local and every AirPlay speaker form one "connected" list sorted by name ignoring case, numbers in numeric order, ties stable. Each tag's icon is an existing SF Symbol, never a bolt (reserved for auto-connect), grey unless the speaker plays, then in its own color.
- **S153** *Observed* (tests "notices: …"; `Notices.swift`, `NoticeList.post`, `NoticeList.clear`; `core/src/policy/speakers.rs`, `MAX_NOTICES`, `NoticeSubject`), and *Required* for the focus sentence (S266). Notices are newest first, one per subject (a repost replaces and moves to the top), at most 4 (the oldest drop off). Clearing removes only the given subjects and reports whether anything changed. Automation notices are per player and carry their settings pane, and privacy panes open System Settings at Local Network, Automation or Microphone. Posting or clearing a notice never opens, raises, activates or focuses a window and never moves the keyboard focus: a focused notice keeps it while others come and go (on Windows by the notice's ID). On the Mac a notice is also a modal alert only when the user just acted in the app (the user origin of S120); one caused from the phone or by AudioSync on its own is only a notice. Screen readers hear a new notice that isn't a repeat (Windows' notice announcer; on the Mac, the preset notice of S265).
- **S154** *Observed* (`AirPlayReconcile.swift:230-256`; tests "local network: …", "local network probe: …"). Local Network hint: none while the engine isn't up; denied access → hint at once; allowed → never; unknown → a soft hint only once the running engine has listed no AirPlay speaker for 20 s. A Bonjour browser waiting or failing on PolicyDenied means denied; ready means allowed; other waits mean unknown; setup and cancel keep the last answer.
- **S155** *Observed* (tests "appearance: …"). Appearance choice: system, light or dark, round-tripped through its stored value; missing or unknown → system, which leaves the app appearance unset; light → aqua, dark → darkAqua.
- **S156** *Observed* (`docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` "Running and testing"; run-tests output "shellcheck 0.11.0 passed (N scripts)", N being the number of listed scripts). Every listed shell script passes the pinned shellcheck version; a missing shellcheck fails the run.
- **S170** *Required* (tests "debug log: …"; `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`). The debug log is off unless `AUDIOSYNC_DEBUG` is exactly `1`; while it is off no message is built and nothing touches the file system for it. When on, the host empties `/tmp/audiosync-debug.log` at launch, writes a first line with the app and macOS versions and the local time, then one line per message, each starting with the UTC time to the millisecond, rounded down (`2026-10-18T17:52:13.456Z`, the Windows app's format). The file grows to at most 50 MB; the line that would pass that is replaced by one saying the log stopped, and later messages are dropped. The path is opened without following a symbolic link and without blocking (a FIFO there can't stall it), and is used only when it is a regular file owned by the user with a single hard link; only then is it emptied and made readable by its owner only. Logging never does file work on the caller's thread: the caller builds the message and stamps the time, and one serial queue writes each whole line unbuffered, in the order the lines were handed over; lines still queued are written when the app exits normally. Building a message still allocates, so audio callbacks must not log (S126, S131).
- **S171** *Required* (tests "devices: …"; S60, S68). The device lists are read afresh from Core Audio on every call, in Core Audio's order: outputs are the devices with at least one output channel, inputs those with at least one input channel. Both leave out a device that reports it is no longer alive (a vanishing device can linger in the list for a moment, and listing it would make its speaker fail to start and be switched off under S69 instead of waiting under S68; a device that doesn't answer stays listed) and any private aggregate device (only the process that made it sees it, so it is the capture's own and never a speaker or a source); an aggregate the user made stays. A device whose name can't be read stays listed with an empty name, which the window shows as an unknown device; an unreadable UID stays empty (callers skip such devices) and an unreadable transport is unknown (0). The default output can be read, but nothing in a session follows it (S145).
- **S211** *Observed* (`WindowRules.swift`, `SourceList`; tests "sources: …"). The source picker lists the whole system first (its entry has the empty ID of S12), then the apps, then the input devices that have a UID, marked as inputs (an input without a UID is left out). A chosen app that isn't running stays listed under its name, sorted among the apps, and a running chosen app is listed once; a chosen input device that is unplugged is not listed.
- **S212** *Observed* (`AppSettings.swift`, `AppSettings.load` and `SettingsStore`; `WindowRules.swift`, `StoredEqualizer`; tests "settings: …", "equalizer storage: …"). Settings are kept in the user defaults under the key names existing installs use, and every saved setting comes back unchanged at the next launch. Nothing stored gives the defaults: the whole system as source (stored as the empty source, S12), master 100, delays 2000 ms. A set of speakers is stored as a sorted list. A master that isn't a finite number starts at 100 and one above 100 is clamped; speaker volumes are clamped to 0 to 100 and delays to 0 to 20 000 ms, and a stored value that isn't a number is dropped (S76, S77). Unreadable groups, IDs or appearance fall back to their defaults. Older stored forms convert once (S75, S77, S179). The equalizer's settings (S24) are stored as one encoded value; nothing stored, data that can't be read, settings with another band count than ten, or a preamp or gain that isn't a finite number give the defaults.
- **S213** *Observed* (`AppSettings.swift`, `RetiredEngineChoice`, `RetiredAirPlayChoice`, `SettingsStore.retiredChoiceNotes`; tests "retired engine choice: …", "retired airplay choice: …", "retired choices: …"). Older builds read two choices that no longer exist: the audio engine (setting `engineImpl`, variable `AUDIOSYNC_ENGINE`) and the AirPlay sender (setting `airplayImpl`, variable `AUDIOSYNC_AIRPLAY`). Neither has any effect. A stored choice is noted once in the debug log at the first launch that finds it and then removed, leaving every other setting; an empty or non-text stored value is removed without a note; a second launch over the same settings logs nothing. A variable still set in the environment can't be removed, so it is noted at every launch. When both the setting and the variable of one choice are set, one line notes both; nothing set, or only empty values, means no note.
- **S214** *Observed* (`WindowRules.swift`, `WindowPlacement`; tests "windows: …"). A window reopens at its saved frame only when the frame is reachable: at least 120 by 60 points of it lie on one display's visible area, enough to grab the title bar. A frame on a display that is gone, or only a sliver of it on screen (40 points), centers the window instead; with no display at all no frame is reachable.
- **S215** *Observed* (`AppLifecycle.swift`, `isLoginItemLaunch`, `answersSecondLaunch`; `Localization.swift`, `streamingStartMessage`; tests "launch: …", "second launch: …", "start error: …"). A launch is a login-item launch only when macOS opens AudioSync at login; the main window opens at every other launch, not at login. A second launch is answered by the running copy, which shows its window, unless that copy is quitting (S111), in which case it doesn't answer and the new copy waits for it to go. A start that fails because the audio core can't be set up says so and asks the user to open the app again, a message of its own, not a capture failure's (S121).
- **S216** *Observed* (`ProcessTapCapture.swift`, `closeCapture`; tests "capture close: …"). Closing a capture stops its IOProc at once; its process tap and the aggregate device built around it are destroyed on the main queue's next turn, after whatever the same turn does next. A source switch therefore makes the new tap before the old one is destroyed. An input device's capture has no tap, so nothing is left for the next turn.
- **S217** *Superseded in part by S272* (the single limit of 6060 bytes and 600 px; the encoding rules hold for both of S272's images). *Observed* (`TrackMetadata.swift`, `jpeg`, `fit`; tests "artwork: …"). Artwork for the receivers is a JPEG of at most `artworkLimit` (6060) bytes, with its longest side at most 600 px and its aspect kept; a smaller image is never scaled up. Within those limits it is as large as fits: each size is tried at three JPEG qualities, best first, before the side steps down (to no less than 64 px), so a tighter byte limit gives a smaller image, and nothing at all comes when not even the smallest size fits. A player's artwork and AudioSync's own icon (an `NSImage`) are encoded the same way, drawn on black since JPEG has no transparency.
- **S272** *Required* (AIRPLAY_SPEC P185). Artwork for AirPlay receivers is encoded twice, by S217's rules (aspect kept, never scaled up, three qualities best first before the side steps down, 64 px floor, black under transparency): a *large* JPEG of at most 1024 px on its longest side and 131 072 bytes (128 KiB), and a *small* one of at most 600 px and 40 960 bytes (40 KiB). The host hands the sender both with the track; when the large one is also within the small limits, the same bytes serve as both. Both hosts follow this rule (the Mac app's `TrackMetadata.swift`, the desktop app's `now_playing.rs`). Cast artwork has its own limits and is not part of it. *Observed:* the 6060 bytes of S217 are not a receiver's limit. They are the largest image whose base64 metadata item still fit one 8 KiB write into the metadata pipe of the AirPlay engine bundled before 2026-10-20, and the number stayed when that pipe went; on a 43-inch TV on the AirPlay 2 path, images of that size (5146 and 5981 bytes) showed visibly soft. Tests to write: "artwork: the large image is at most 1024 px a side and 128 KiB", "artwork: the small image is at most 600 px a side and 40 KiB", "artwork: an image within the small limits is one encoding for both".
- **S218** *Observed* (`ReceiverInfo.swift`, `ReceiverInfo`; tests "receiver info: …"). What AirPlay receivers with a screen show. A playing track of the source's player goes out with its fields as the player gave them (blank ones included) and its artwork. With the whole system as source, the player seen playing last since the source was chosen keeps its track and artwork while paused (`NowPlayingMonitor.LastPlayed`), until another player plays (a playing player always beats a paused one), that player stops or quits, or the source changes; then no track counts, and another player's paused track doesn't take its place. With a player as source, its paused track still shows, but another player's paused track doesn't. A paused track still shows while its player runs, through any number of readings that get an error, no answer within 2 s or a paused state with an empty track name (`NowPlaying.swift`, `EmptyReadings`; tests "now playing: …"); it goes at once when the player says it stopped, another track is read, the player quits or macOS refuses AudioSync's Apple Events to it, and after `dropAfter` (3) readings in a row that play something without a name. A playing track goes after `dropAfter` empty readings of any kind in a row. The debug log names each drop and its reason. With no track that counts, every field is filled: title "AudioSync", the source's name as the source picker shows it as the artist ("All System Audio" for the whole system, the app's or input device's name, never a bundle ID; a stand-in when there is no usable name), and a short album label, with AudioSync's icon as artwork. A Music track without artwork gets AudioSync's icon. Spotify's artwork arrives after its track: while it may still come, what is shown stays, and after `artworkWait` (5 s) without it AudioSync's icon takes its place; waiting changes nothing but that wait. After polling starts, the host waits up to `readingWait` (3 s) for the first Now Playing reading; a current reading, or none coming because nothing polls, is not waited for.
- **S219** *Observed* (`ReceiverInfo.swift`, `SentTrackInfo`; `NativeAirPlayModel.swift`, `NativeNowPlaying`; tests "sent track info: …", "native track info: …"). What the host hands AudioSync's own sender, so a receiver never shows one track with another's cover. A new track goes out with its artwork; another artist or album counts as a new track. The sender drops the artwork when the track changes, so the next track with the same cached image sends that image again, and a new track whose artwork isn't there yet goes out without any. The same track again (a model update, a recheck) sends nothing, another image for the same track goes out, and a new sender (a resend) gets the track and its artwork again. With no track, AudioSync's labels go out with its icon, which isn't sent again for the same fields; a track without artwork after one with gets the icon at once, and artwork that comes for a track shown with the icon goes out. With no icon to give, the fields go out alone and the icon follows once there is one. While a track's artwork is on its way (S218), the track goes out alone with the time the wait ends; a look during the wait sends nothing and keeps that time, artwork that arrives in time goes out, and otherwise the icon goes out once when the wait is over.
- **S220** *Observed* (`LocalDelay.swift`, `DelayFieldDraft`, `fieldText`; tests "delay field: …", "delay field cue: …", "delay field quit: …", "delay field input: …"). A delay field (an offset of S175 or the AirPlay delay of S178, as S181 shows them) starts out showing the saved value and keeps typed text as a draft: typing applies nothing. Return applies the typed value once, clamped to its range and shown clamped (the AirPlay delay to 20 000 ms); a focus loss after it applies nothing more. Text that can't be read, or the saved value typed again, applies nothing, and unreadable text shows the saved value again. A step applies on top of the typed value when it reads, else on the saved value, once, so a later focus loss applies nothing more; at the range's end a step applies nothing. A new saved value shows when nothing is being typed. The field marks a draft: none while it shows the saved value or the saved value as typed; typing shows one; Return, Escape and a step clear it (unreadable text keeps it until Return restores the saved value); a saved value changing under the typing keeps it. Quitting drops every draft, so a delay typed but not applied at quit is neither applied nor saved. Only digits after one leading "+" or "−" can be typed: any other keystroke, or a paste holding anything else, is blocked before it shows (a letter after digits doesn't show). Only a number outside the field's range gets a red outline, with a hint naming the range and the value Return applies; a negative AirPlay delay stays marked until Return applies 0.
- **S221** *Observed* (`LocalDelay.swift`, `DelayPanels`; tests "delay panels: …"). Each speaker row with a delay opens a panel holding its delay field (S220). Return applies its row's typing once and leaves every panel open; Command-S applies every open panel's typing and leaves them open; leaving the field for another control (Tab, another field, a click on any control) applies its typing once and leaves every panel open; a click on a step of that same panel instead leaves the focus in the field, and the step builds on the typing and applies once. A click on a row's delay or its box (its open panel's blank space included) applies that row's typing and closes its panel, and on a closed row's box opens its panel without applying anything. A click on another row applies the open panel's typing first and leaves that panel open. The note under a list belongs to the last row: a click on it toggles that row's panel, after applying any other panel's typing; under a row without a delay, or with no row above it, it counts as outside. A click outside every speaker row applies each readable typing and closes every panel (unreadable text shows the saved delay again); Escape drops every typing and closes every panel, and the focus leaving the field after it applies nothing.
- **S222** *Observed* (`NativeAirPlayModel.swift`, `NativeAirPlayID`, `NativeAirPlayModel`, `NativeSenderStarts`; `NativeReceiver.swift`; tests "native airplay: …", "own receiver: …", "EC7 native airplay: …", "EC12 native airplay: …", "EC13 native airplay: …"). The host's side of AudioSync's own AirPlay sender (docs/AIRPLAY_SPEC.md). IDs: a receiver ID becomes the app ID `native:<receiver ID>` and back, instance IDs with colons included, and survives a speaker key (S149); an ID older versions saved is never taken for a native receiver, and a speaker saved as on under such an ID, not moved yet (S204), neither starts nor holds up streaming. Rows list, by name, only receivers that can play or need a code (PIN or password), shown as asking for a code (S106); nothing else, and never this Mac's own AirPlay Receiver: a receiver whose name is exactly the computer's name, ignoring case, is the Mac, and with no computer name known nothing is hidden. Only a receiver able to play holds local outputs back (S176). Nothing joins while not streaming; while streaming, the able join, the ones needing a PIN or password that the user didn't turn on by a click go off with a notice (auto-connect and restored switches never pair or prompt, S223), the rest wait; an added speaker stays, and leaves when switched off or when streaming stops. Playing is reported once. A speaker that plays on after its announcement lapsed stays listed and chosen; a lost speaker the sender retries stays on; gone and not playing, it leaves the list and holds nothing back. An error the sender won't retry turns the speaker off, with why; events of a removed speaker are passed over; local outputs line up again only when L (S178) changes. The first reconcile starts the sender (S101). A failed start is told once, another failure in a row posts no second notice, a start that works ends the failures, and a new failure after it is told again; a running sender shows no problem. A sender that stopped after an internal error, or whose failure event arrives, is dropped, doesn't restart by itself and shows as failed with Retry, which starts a new sender at once. For the Local Network hint (S154) the sender is up only after a start that worked and until it stops. Debug log lines name a found receiver (ID, model, availability, hints), a changed one with its reason, a gone one (by name when it was listed), a retrying state, an error, latency and timeline figures, and a failure with where a panic happened (S205). *Required* (2026-10-26, both hosts; Mac: `NativeAirPlayModel.swift`, `plan`, `goneUnplayed`, tests "native airplay: a retrying speaker whose receiver is gone …", "native airplay: a speaker playing on after its announcement lapsed leaves the sender once it is lost"; Windows: `desktop/src/audio/airplay.rs`, `reconcile`, `gone_unplayed`, `take_state`; `desktop/src/adapter.rs`, `airplay_listed`; tests "a retrying speaker whose receiver is gone leaves the sender and joins again when announced", "a speaker playing on after its announcement lapsed leaves once it is lost", "a speaker playing on after its announcement lapsed leaves once its session restarts", "a retrying speaker whose receiver is gone is named by no notice"): a speaker gone and not playing leaves the sender too, once discovery withdraws it, or once it is lost or its session starts over after its announcement lapsed. It stays on, its retries stop, no notice names it (a retrying notice it had goes with its row), and it joins again when it is announced able to play. Reason: a receiver powered off mid-stream lost its row about 40 s later, yet the sender kept retrying it and raised retrying notices for a speaker the list no longer showed. A stereo pair or home theater group in the sender keeps its row while it reconnects (P176), so it stays. *Required* (2026-10-27, Windows; `desktop/src/audio/airplay.rs`, `turn_off_uninvited`, tests "a speaker saved on that now asks for a pin goes off with a notice at a launch", "a speaker saved on that now asks for a password goes off with a notice at a launch"): while streaming, a speaker needing a PIN or a password that the user didn't turn on by a click this run goes off with a notice; it never stays on and silent, and it is never paired or prompted on its own (S223). Reason: a speaker restored as on that now asked for a PIN stayed on and silent at a launch.
- **S223** *Observed* (`NativeAirPlayModel.swift`, `NativeAccessStage`, `NativePrompt`, `NativeTurnOff`; `WindowRules.swift`, `PromptWithdrawal`; tests "native pairing: …", "prompt withdrawal: …"). Pairing and passwords for AudioSync's own sender. A click on a receiver able to play just joins; on an Apple TV asking for a PIN it starts a pairing (the TV shows a PIN and a prompt is queued); on a receiver with a password it asks for the password, unless it was given one earlier this run, and then it just joins. While pairing or prompting the speaker waits instead of going off, and no session or second pairing starts for it, even when it is listed as able to play or a session of it is refused meanwhile, since a new `/pair-pin-start` makes the receiver show a new code. A speaker that asks for a code every time (flags bit 3) goes off whenever it is listed asking and is neither playing nor switched on by hand this run: when it appears, also on from the last run, and when a listed one starts asking (tests "every time: …"). Prompts come oldest first, one at a time. An empty PIN counts as Cancel; a PIN is otherwise ASCII digits only, and anything else asks again; a PIN goes to its pairing, and a wrong one starts a new pairing (the TV shows a PIN again) and asks again. Once paired the speaker waits for the sender's new listing, then joins when listed as able to play; refused again right after pairing, it goes off instead of pairing in a loop. With its password a receiver joins; a wrong password asks again while the user's attempt runs; an empty password counts as Cancel. A paired Apple TV just joins. Within the user's attempt a refused stored pairing pairs again; refused without the user's click, or later once it has played (which ends the attempt), it goes off with a notice instead of prompting uninvited. Cancel turns the speaker off and cancels a running pairing once. A pairing that can't start, a PIN the pairing didn't take, a pairing turned down or failing for another reason turns the speaker off; a pairing that needs the password asks for it and pairs again with it. Turning a speaker off cancels the pairing that waits for its PIN and takes back its open prompt; a PIN request nobody waits for cancels its pairing; a pairing that ends after its speaker went off, or an answer for a speaker turned off meanwhile, is passed over. A prompt its pairing waits for stays open (a password prompt while its speaker waits for it); a pairing that fails under its open PIN prompt, or a speaker that plays under it on stored credentials, takes the prompt back once. A prompt taken back is no answer, a PIN typed into it goes nowhere, and its answer never counts for the attempt that asks again, whose prompt opens next. A prompt taken back closes at once when it is the frontmost modal window; under another alert it waits, without a second retry when taken back again, and closes once that alert ends; a prompt that went meanwhile, or one already answered, stops the retries, so the next prompt is never closed in its place. Log lines name the receiver, never a PIN or password.
- **S224** *Observed* (`NativeAirPlayModel.swift`, `NativeAirPlayPairings`; tests "pairing store: …"). The sender's stored pairings are one file, `airplay-pairings.dat`, which holds them in plain form by design, so it lives in a `Pairings` folder of its own inside AudioSync's application support folder: that folder is created for its owner only and marked excluded from backups (also when it exists), while AudioSync's own folder stays in backups. A file where older builds kept it, directly in AudioSync's folder, moves into the `Pairings` folder unchanged and still for its owner only; with no older file nothing moves and nothing is created; once moved, the next launch moves nothing; with both files, the one in the folder is kept and the older one is left alone. When the core opens this file or the AirPlay 2 identity file beside it, a file other accounts may read or write is set to mode 0600 and still used; a symbolic link in the file's place is not followed and counts as an unreadable file; a pairings file another account owns is ignored as an unreadable one is, and an identity file another account owns is ignored and replaced at once with a new identity. The host is told how private each file was found (owner-only or missing, made owner-only, or still open to other accounts because its mode couldn't be changed) and writes it to its debug log; a file that wasn't read (another account owns it, it isn't a regular file, or opening it failed) reports nothing, and one made owner-only before its read failed still reports that.
- **S225** *Observed* (`Tests/soakrules.swift`, `SoakRules`; soak rules suite, every check). The soak harness's memory rule. A run's memory growth is the last window's footprint minus the level the run settled at: the median of the last three windows inside the warm-up (of those there are, the mean of two when only two), the warm-up being the first 2 minutes, or the first half of a run shorter than 4 minutes. With no window in the warm-up growth counts from the first window; no windows, or a single one, mean no growth. A climb that ends within the warm-up counts as nothing, a leak shows in full from the warm-up's end, and a spike in the warm-up (even in its last window) can neither hide a later leak nor fail a flat run. A run passes when its growth is at most max(2 × b, b + 5) MB, b being the baseline run's growth, a negative one counting as 0.
- **S228** *Observed* (`SpeakerNames.swift`, `SpeakerNames`, `SpeakerRename`; `AppSettings.swift`; tests "speaker names: …", "speaker rename: …"). The names the user gives speakers. A speaker shows its system name (the one macOS or AirPlay discovery reports) until renamed. A typed name is trimmed, line breaks inside it become spaces, and it is cut to 60 characters; an empty name, or the system name typed again, brings back the system name. Names are kept by speaker key (S149) under `speakerNamesByKey`, so they outlive a reconnect and a relaunch; with no name left the key is removed, and a stored entry that isn't a name is skipped. A name is display-only (S147): it shows in the speaker's row, the menu bar panel, notices, alerts and VoiceOver labels, never reaches the engine, and while renamed the system name stays in the row's tooltip and its VoiceOver label, in the window and the panel. One speaker is renamed at a time: Return or the focus leaving applies the typing once, Escape drops it, starting another speaker's rename applies the open one first, and a speaker that leaves its list mid-rename applies its typing, its rename not opening again when it returns.

## 18. Test map

Check names as `Tests/run-tests.sh` prints them. Checks printed more than once are listed once. `run-tests.sh` also compiles the soak and benchmark harness (`Tests/soak.sh`, `Tests/bench.sh`) without running it; those long runs are outside this map.

### shellcheck

| Check | Clauses |
|---|---|
| shellcheck 0.11.0 passed (N scripts) | S156 |

### ring buffer

Removed on 2026-10-19 with the Swift engine's ring (`Tests/ringtest.swift`). What it checked, the steady delay, the floor at one block, stalls, live delay changes, two readers, short gaps, drift both ways and the event counters, is the core's reader tests now: `core/src/reader.rs`, `core/tests/drift.rs` and `core/tests/reader_props.rs`, which name the clauses they cover (S41 to S59, S158, S159).

### logic

The bundled engine's checks were removed with it on 2026-10-20: its config file (`config: …`), process states (`engine: …`), output list (`outputs: …`), pipe playback and track info (`pipe: …`, `track info: …`), reconcile, pairing and volume writes (`reconcile: …`, `pairing: …`, `pairing queue: …`, `volume writes: …` and the AirPlay `volume: …` checks), quit sequence (`shutdown: …`), its sleep and restart cases (`EC3 sleep: …`, `EC3 wake: …`, `EC7 engine restart: …`), its EC10, EC11 and EC12 checks, and the bundled engine's AirPlay delay field. Their clauses are retired (see the note at the top).

### core (`Tests/coretest.swift`)

The bundled engine's checks were removed with it on 2026-10-20: the metadata pipe (`metadata pipe: …`) and the sync rule's checks against its output list (`delay: …` for a chosen speaker, listed or not, and "EC12 network loss: local speakers stop waiting for an AirPlay speaker that is gone").

The pipe feed's own checks were removed with it on 2026-10-20 (the `fifo: …` checks, the EC7 reopen check and the session's pipe feed rows); the suite checks the app's wrappers of the core. It was the fifo suite, `Tests/fifotest.swift`, until it took its present name the same day.
From 2026-10-19 to 2026-10-20 the suite ran the app's pipe feed, `CoreFifoFeed`, besides the app's other wrappers of the core; the equalizer, frame count, delay and sync rule rows came from the logic suite, where they ran on the Swift engine. "ring: reset clears history" went with the Swift ring: every session gets a new core ring (S39, `core/src/ring.rs`). The check of a stop inside a chunk's last frame went too: the core wrote at most 512 bytes at a time, which a pipe takes whole, and its tests drove that case with a fake pipe (S93).

### core parity

Removed on 2026-10-19 with the Swift engine (`Tests/paritytest.swift`): with one engine there is nothing to compare. Its checks of the core's parts through the app's wrappers moved to the fifo suite (the core suite since 2026-10-20): "rust: fifo reopen resumes at the live edge (S169)" became the fifo suite's EC7 check, and the two pipe stall checks moved there with the "rust: " prefix dropped from their names; all three went with the FIFO feed on 2026-10-20.

### pipe start (`--hardware`, `Tests/pipestart.swift`)

Removed on 2026-10-20 with the bundled engine: it ran a private engine whose only output was its fifo output and checked that the pipe's audio came out of it, and that pipe playback came back no later than at the start after a pause, a stop and a writer that closed (the fifo feed's S83 and S88, and the retired S96 and S99).

### large delays: core (`cargo test` in `core/`)

The checks for S187's large increases and for S47 and S48 at the largest hold-back (`core/tests/render_large_delay.rs`). The capture writes 512-frame blocks at 48 kHz on a simulated clock and stamps them; the device, at 48 or 44.1 kHz, pulls 512-frame callbacks with the time each reads at and outputs at, as the Mac's render callback gives them; the ring is the Mac's 4 608 000 frames. A check passes when the output sounds within 0.3 s of when it should, stays audible after that, and ends with its reader lagging the new delay within 50 ms.

| Test | Clauses |
|---|---|
| an_output_started_at_20_s_sounds_20_s_after_the_capture_and_plays_on | S77, S175 |
| a_change_from_0_to_20_s_sounds_at_once_at_the_new_delay | S187, S159 |
| a_change_from_2_to_15_or_20_s_sounds_at_once_at_the_new_delay | S187, S159 |
| an_increase_of_exactly_5_s_inserts_its_silence_and_one_frame_more_restarts | S47, S187, S159 |
| a_change_to_20_s_before_20_s_were_captured_sounds_once_they_were | S187, S159, S45 |
| an_increase_of_2_s_still_inserts_its_silence | S47, S187 |
| a_change_from_20_s_to_10_s_lands_and_plays_on | S48, S187 |

### fades: Mac

The Mac's checks for S182 to S191 were written first and failed against a scaffold that answered as the Mac code of that day behaved (a stop stopped capture first and every output at once, nothing faded and nothing waited); they pass now that the Mac app renders through the core's renderer (`AscRenderer`) and waits for the fades, and are listed in the logic and engine tables above, as "fade: …" and the three "\<device\>" rows. The fifo suite's two fade checks, the pipe's fade-in after an open and its fade-out on a stop request, went with the FIFO feed on 2026-10-20.

By ear on real AirPlay receivers, outside these suites: with a speaker playing, a stop and a source change make no click on the speaker when the engine stops or the native sender flushes it, and the first sound after a start or a source change fades in (S189).

### render start: core (`cargo test` in `core/`)

The checks for S206 and S207 (`core/tests/render_start.rs`). The capture writes fixed blocks at 48 kHz on its own clock and the device pulls fixed callbacks on its own, both exact, over several phases between the two clocks, with the output switched on in a running session and started with a new one; the ring holds a constant 0.5. A converting output's clock also runs 100 ppm fast or slow: for one second after the start in the start checks, and for 30 seconds in the check of an earliest output on a clock off; the few-phase checks stamp the blocks and callbacks with their times (S207) and run for 60 seconds, 100 and 300 ppm off. The late-wake checks run a Windows endpoint in step with 10 ms packets, one wake of which comes a period late; the check of two outputs writes a ramp, so the frames both play at the same instant can be compared.

### device volume: Mac

The Mac's checks for S192 to S202 were written first and failed against the Mac code of that day (the first one reproduced the quiet MacBook speakers at 13 %); they pass now and are listed in the logic table as "device volume". They drive the host's Core Audio access through a fake that keeps a volume and a mute per element, records the listeners and every write, and notifies a listened control, so no real device is read or written; the decisions come from the core (`asc_device_volume_decide`, S196, S197, S201).

By hand on the Mac, outside these suites: set the built-in speakers to 13 % in Audio MIDI Setup, switch them on in AudioSync and see the hint; click Set to 75% and hear the level rise, with the hint gone and the system volume at 75 %; check the hint and the button in light and dark appearance and with VoiceOver (S193, S198, S200).

## 19. Windows updater

The Windows app updates itself from the same Sparkle appcast the Mac app reads, through its own small updater written in Rust. This section is the whole behavior; a writer needs nothing else. Sources named below are public documentation: Sparkle's "Publishing an update" (sparkle-project.org/documentation/publishing/) and its "Documentation" basic setup on EdDSA keys (sparkle-project.org/documentation/), WinSparkle's "Publishing updates" guide for the Windows values of `sparkle:os` (winsparkle.org/guides/publishing-updates/), RFC 8032 for Ed25519, and Microsoft Learn's MoveFileExW page (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/winbase/nf-winbase-movefileexw), its WinHTTP overview (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/winhttp/about-winhttp), its PE format page (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/debug/pe-format), its SmartScreen reputation page (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/package-and-deploy/smartscreen-reputation), its Smart App Control overview (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/smart-app-control/overview), the Zone.Identifier stream in MS-FSCC (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/windows_protocols/ms-fscc/6e3f7352-d11c-4d76-8c39-2516a9df36e8), IAttachmentExecute::Save (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/shobjidl_core/nf-shobjidl_core-iattachmentexecute-save), Microsoft Defender Antivirus protection features (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/defender-endpoint/configure-protection-features-microsoft-defender-antivirus) and System Error Codes 0 to 499 (learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/debug/system-error-codes--0-499-). No updater project's code was read for it, and none may be copied from.

- **S293** *Required.* *Scope.* The updater exists in the Windows app only, never on Linux (the AppImage is updated by hand) and never on the Mac (Sparkle does it there). It runs only in a release build: a build the release CI job makes, marked at compile time, the same build whose x64 and ARM64 zips `RELEASE.md` attaches; a local `cargo build`, a test build and any other build never check, and show neither updater menu item. The feed is the one appcast the Mac reads, at the URL in `Info.plist` `SUFeedURL` (today `releases/latest/download/appcast.xml` on the project's GitHub releases), compiled into the exe. Every crate the updater adds (an XML reader, Ed25519, zip) is permissive, passes cargo-deny and gets its entry in the Windows notices through `scripts/windows_notices.py`. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S294** *Required.* *Fetching.* The appcast and the download go over HTTPS only, through the platform's own HTTP stack (WinHTTP, reached through the `windows` crate the app already links), so the system's proxy settings and certificate store apply and no HTTP crate is added. Redirects are followed (a GitHub release download answers with a redirect to its file host) up to 5 hops, and only to `https:` URLs; a redirect to anything else fails the fetch. No cookies, credentials or tokens are sent. The user agent is `AudioSync/<version> (Windows; <arch>)` with arch `x64` or `arm64`. Limits: 15 s to connect, 30 s for the whole appcast, at most 1 MiB of appcast (more is a failed fetch), and a download that receives no byte for 30 s fails. Any HTTP status other than 200 after redirects is a failed fetch. Source: Microsoft Learn, About WinHTTP; AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S295** *Required.* *Reading the appcast.* The appcast is RSS 2.0 with Sparkle's namespace: a `channel` holding `item`s. A document carrying a DOCTYPE is refused as a whole, and no entity is expanded, so the reader never fetches or grows anything the document names. From each item the updater reads: `sparkle:version` and `sparkle:shortVersionString`, from the item's own child elements, or else from the attributes of the same name on its enclosure (Sparkle accepts both and recommends the elements); `sparkle:channel` and `sparkle:minimumSystemVersion` if present; and every `enclosure` with its `url`, `length`, `sparkle:os` and `sparkle:edSignature` attributes. An item that lacks what S296 needs, or holds a malformed value, is skipped on its own and logged; the other items still count. Source: Sparkle, Publishing an update; AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S296** *Required.* *Picking the update.* The app's own architecture is the one the exe was built for (x86-64 gives `windows-x64`, AArch64 gives `windows-arm64`); an x64 build running under emulation on an ARM64 PC stays x64. A candidate is an enclosure whose `sparkle:os` equals that value exactly; `windows` alone, `windows-x86`, `macos` and an enclosure without `sparkle:os` (a Mac item) never are. An item with a `sparkle:channel` is skipped (only the default channel counts), and so is one whose `sparkle:minimumSystemVersion` names a Windows version above the running one (both compared as dotted numbers such as `10.0.22000`). Versions compare by `sparkle:shortVersionString` against the exe's own version (the `desktop/Cargo.toml` version, the same string as the Mac's `CFBundleShortVersionString` for that release): split on dots, each part a decimal number, a missing part counting as 0, so `0.7.10` is above `0.7.9` and `0.8` equals `0.8.0`; a part that isn't a decimal number skips that item. The update is the candidate with the highest version strictly above the running one. None (no matching enclosure, or only the same or older versions) means nothing happens: no notice, no download, one debug log line, and on a manual check (S305) the "up to date" notice. Source: Sparkle, Publishing an update; WinSparkle, Publishing updates (the `sparkle:os` values); AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S297** *Required.* *Asking first.* An update found shows a notice in the app's notice list (S153) saying which version is available and which one runs, with two buttons: Download and Install, and Not Now. Nothing is downloaded before the user selects Download and Install. Not Now closes the notice; the launch check (S305) offers it again at the next due check, a manual check at once. A newer update found later replaces the notice. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S298** *Required.* *Downloading.* The enclosure's file goes into a new, empty folder the updater creates in the user's temp folder (`%TEMP%`), named `AudioSync-update-` plus a random suffix, and nowhere else. The download stops and counts as failed when it passes the enclosure's `length`, or 256 MiB without a `length`, and when it ends shorter than a given `length`. While it runs, the notice shows the progress as a percentage when the size is known and offers Cancel; Cancel deletes the folder. Streaming goes on untouched throughout: the updater runs on its own worker thread, never on the UI thread or an audio thread, and nothing it does waits on audio. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S299** *Required.* *The signature, before anything else.* The downloaded file must carry a valid Sparkle EdDSA signature before it is opened as a zip or anything leaves the temp folder. `sparkle:edSignature` is the base64 of a 64-byte Ed25519 signature (RFC 8032, plain Ed25519, not the prehashed Ed25519ph) over the file's exact bytes; the public key is the base64 of a 32-byte Ed25519 key, the same value the Mac ships as `SUPublicEDKey` in `Info.plist`, compiled into the exe. A missing attribute, base64 that doesn't decode to 64 bytes, or a signature that fails verification is a bad signature: the file and its folder are deleted at once, the notice says the download didn't pass its check and nothing was installed, and the debug log names the version and the URL. A test build may verify with a throwaway test key; a release build only ever holds the release key. Source: Sparkle, Documentation (EdDSA keys, base64 public key, signed archives); RFC 8032; AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S300** *Required.* *Unzipping.* Only a verified file is opened, as a zip, and only inside its temp folder. Every entry name must be a plain file name at the top of the archive: an entry with a folder part, `..`, a drive letter, a leading slash or backslash, or a name that is empty is refused, and so is the whole archive. The archive must hold exactly one `audiosync.exe` (names compared without case); it may hold `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt`; every other entry is skipped, never written. Entries are written to the temp folder with at most 256 MiB unpacked in total. The unpacked exe must be a PE image whose machine field matches the app's own architecture (x64 or ARM64, S296); any other is refused like a bad archive. A refused archive deletes the temp folder and is reported as a broken download, changing nothing. Nothing unpacked is ever run from the temp folder. Source: Microsoft Learn, PE format (the machine field); AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S301** *Required.* *Swapping the running exe.* The install folder is the folder of the running `audiosync.exe` (its resolved path, links followed). Windows lets a running program's file be renamed within its volume but not overwritten or deleted, and a rename across volumes is a copy, so the swap works by renames inside the install folder, in this order: (1) delete an `audiosync.exe.new` left over, then create `audiosync.exe.new` exclusively (the create fails if the file exists again) and copy the new exe into it, flushed to disk; (2) delete an `audiosync.exe.bak` left from an earlier update (never the running image: a process that has swapped once runs from that `.bak` and never reaches this step again, S303); (3) rename the running `audiosync.exe` to `audiosync.exe.bak`; (4) rename `audiosync.exe.new` to `audiosync.exe`. A `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt` from the zip goes through the same `.new` and `.bak` steps, after the exe, and is restored with it. Each rename completes on disk before the next step (write-through). After step 4 the old version keeps running from its `.bak` file until the user relaunches (S303). The temp folder is deleted when the swap ends, whichever way. Source: Microsoft Learn, MoveFileExW (same-volume rename, copy across volumes, replace and write-through flags, delete permission); AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S302** *Required.* *Failures, restore and report.* A failure at any step of S301 undoes what that update did, in reverse order: the `.new` files are deleted; once step 4 ran, the new `audiosync.exe` is deleted; and once step 3 ran, `audiosync.exe.bak` is renamed back to `audiosync.exe`; then the notice says what failed, in plain words, and that the running version stays. The cases: no write permission on the install folder (step 1 is refused, for example under Program Files) offers Open Release Page, which opens the project's latest release page in the default browser so the user can download the zip by hand; a disk-full error while downloading, unpacking or copying deletes every partial file and says there wasn't enough space; step 1 unable to delete a leftover `.new` or its exclusive create failing, or step 2 or 3 refused once step 1 succeeded, counts as the exe being in use by another copy of AudioSync (for example in another user's session) and says to close the other copies and try again, with Try Again; offline or a failed fetch on the launch check is quiet (one debug log line, S305), on a manual check a notice says AudioSync couldn't reach the update server. If the restore itself fails, the notice names the `.bak` file and asks the user to rename it back to `audiosync.exe`; this is the one case that leaves the folder without its exe. Source: Microsoft Learn, MoveFileExW; AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S303** *Required.* *Relaunch on the user's click only.* After a swap the notice says the new version is installed and takes effect after a relaunch, with Relaunch Now. Nothing restarts on its own. Relaunch Now starts the new `audiosync.exe` with a marker naming this process and, once the new instance signals that it started (S314), stops streaming as quitting does, saves the settings and quits. The new instance waits up to 10 s for the named process to end before it claims the session's single-instance lock, so it never hands its launch over to the quitting one, and it reads the settings file only after that process ended or the 10 s wait ran out, so it reads what the old one saved. If the new exe can't be started or gives no start signal (S314), the old one stays running, restores the `.bak` as in S302: it deletes the new `audiosync.exe` first, then renames its own running `.bak` file back to `audiosync.exe`, and reports it. Without a click, the next normal start runs the new version. *Swapped, not relaunched.* Once a swap has completed in a process, that process holds the installed version and offers no update again: every later check in it, manual or at launch, fetches nothing and shows this Relaunch Now notice again, naming the installed version. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S304** *Required.* *Leftovers.* At launch the updater deletes any `audiosync.exe.new` or `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt.new` in its install folder and any `AudioSync-update-` folder in the temp folder, and logs each. It keeps `audiosync.exe.bak`, the one previous version a user can go back to by hand, until the next update replaces it. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S305** *Required.* *When it checks.* At launch, at most once a day: the check runs when no successful check is stored, when 24 hours or more passed since the stored one, or when the stored time lies in the future (a clock moved back). Only a check that fetched and read the appcast stores its time, in the settings file, so an offline launch tries again at the next launch. The File menu holds Check for Updates Automatically, a check item next to Launch at Sign-In, on by default and saved in the settings; off, no launch check runs. Help > Check for Updates… checks at once whatever the switch and the time say, and always answers: an update offers S297's notice, none says the running version is the latest, a failure says so (S302). In a process that has already swapped, both checks answer with S303's Relaunch Now notice instead. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S306** *Required.* *Never silent.* No download, unpack or swap happens without the user's click on Download and Install, and no relaunch without Relaunch Now. Every outcome after that click ends in a notice: installed, or what failed. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S307** *Required.* *SmartScreen, unverified.* A file the app's own HTTP client writes carries no Mark of the Web, so the relaunched exe is expected to start without the SmartScreen prompt a browser download gets; this is not verified. The first PC sitting with a real update runs the relaunch once and records whether a prompt appeared. If one did, the updater stops swapping: Download and Install is replaced by Open Release Page (S302), so the user downloads through the browser and gets SmartScreen's usual prompt there. Smart App Control, when on, may block an unsigned exe either way; only code signing would change that, which is out of scope. Source: Microsoft Learn, SmartScreen reputation; AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S308** *Required.* *Text, themes and keyboard.* Every word the updater shows or speaks comes from `desktop/ui/strings.slint`: the two menu items, the notices of S297, S298, S299, S300, S302, S303, S305, S314, S315 and S316, and the buttons Download and Install, Not Now, Cancel, Try Again, Open Release Page and Relaunch Now. The notices use the existing notice styling and brand tokens only, so text reaches at least 4.5:1 in light and dark. Tab reaches every updater button with a visible focus, Enter or Space presses it, and a screen reader hears each notice's text and each button's name. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S309** *Required.* *Debug log.* With the debug log on, the updater writes one line per step: the check (launch or manual, due or skipped and why), the appcast fetched (bytes, items read, items skipped and why), the update picked or none, the download (URL host, bytes, time), the signature result, the unpack result, each swap step and its error code, a restore, the relaunch, and each leftover removed. No line holds a path under the user's profile beyond the file name. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S310** *Required.* *The Mac keeps reading the same appcast.* Sparkle documents only partial support for macOS and non-macOS items in one appcast and recommends separate feeds; the advisor chose one feed. So before the first release whose appcast carries Windows items, the Mac app is pointed at that appcast once and must offer the Mac item only. If it offers a Windows item or fails on the feed, the Windows items move to a second appcast, `appcast-windows.xml`, attached to the same release next to `appcast.xml` (so its URL is `releases/latest/download/appcast-windows.xml`), and S293's feed URL becomes that one; the rest of this section stays as it is. Source: Sparkle, Publishing an update (items for several platforms); AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S311** *Required.* *The release side.* A release that carries the Windows zips adds to its `appcast.xml`, next to the Mac item, one item per Windows architecture, each with one enclosure: its zip's download URL and byte length, `sparkle:os` `windows-x64` or `windows-arm64`, and `sparkle:edSignature` made over that zip with the same private key that signs the Mac zip. Each Windows item carries the release's `sparkle:shortVersionString` and the same `sparkle:version` as the Mac item. Each zip holds `audiosync.exe` and `THIRD-PARTY-NOTICES.txt` at its top level, as `RELEASE.md` builds them. A release without Windows zips has no Windows items, so Windows users of the older version see no update. Source: Sparkle, Publishing an update; WinSparkle, Publishing updates; AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-26).
- **S314** *Required.* *The new version's first start.* Relaunch Now (S303) starts the new `audiosync.exe` directly as a child process of the running one, never through the shell's open action, so the old process holds a handle to it and can end it. The new instance's first step, before it waits for the old process and before it reads the settings file (which it reads only once the old process ended or the 10 s wait of S303 ran out), is to signal the old process that it started, through a signal named after the old process's ID, which the marker carries. The old process keeps streaming while it waits, up to 15 s, a span that leaves room for a first start from a cold disk plus Defender's scan of a file it has never seen, yet answers the click within a short wait; on the signal it stops streaming, saves the settings and quits as S303 says. If the new process ends first, or 15 s pass without the signal (Windows security refused to run it, it failed while loading, or a prompt held it), the old process ends the new process if it still runs, restores the `.bak` as S303 does (a new `audiosync.exe` already gone counts as deleted), keeps running and streaming, records the version as failed (S316), and the notice says the new version couldn't start and the running version stays, with Open Release Page. A new version that signals and then fails later in its own start-up is out of the updater's reach: `audiosync.exe.bak` stays next to it (S304), and the old version comes back by renaming it by hand. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-27).
- **S315** *Required.* *Windows security during the download, the unpack and the swap.* What Windows does: a file counts as downloaded from the internet when it carries a `Zone.Identifier` alternate data stream (the Mark of the Web), which the downloading app adds, typically through attachment services, whose save step may also run virus scanners that can delete or alter the file; Microsoft Defender Antivirus scans files continuously (real-time protection) and can remove one it detects, after which file calls report ERROR_VIRUS_INFECTED (225) or ERROR_VIRUS_DELETED (226) or find no file; a process that holds a file open without sharing makes other opens fail with ERROR_SHARING_VIOLATION (32) or ERROR_LOCK_VIOLATION (33). What the updater does: (1) It neither adds, reads, edits nor removes a `Zone.Identifier` stream and calls no attachment service; S301 writes the verified bytes into `.new`, so the installed exe carries a mark only if Windows or a security product adds one, and S307 covers what a mark would mean for the first start. (2) A sharing or lock violation on a file the updater opens, renames or deletes in S298 to S301 is tried again after 250 ms, 500 ms, 1 s, 2 s and 4 s, at most five more tries, on the updater's worker thread: the doubling waits ride out a scan that holds the file for a moment without spinning on it, and five tries keep the whole wait under 8 s so the notice still comes promptly; still locked after that, the step fails as S302 classifies it. No other error is tried again. (3) Error 225 or 226 from any file step, or a file the updater has just written that is missing at its next step (the downloaded zip before S299, the unpacked exe before S301 step 1, `audiosync.exe.new` before step 4, the new `audiosync.exe` at Relaunch Now), counts as blocked by Windows security: nothing is tried again, the swap is undone as in S302 (a file already gone counts as deleted), the temp folder is deleted, the version is recorded as blocked (S316), and the notice says Windows security blocked the update file, nothing was installed and the running version stays, with Open Release Page. The notice never asks the user to turn protection off or to add an exclusion. (4) A process that has swapped and quits without Relaunch Now (Quit, sign-out, shutdown) first checks that `audiosync.exe` is still in its install folder; if Windows security removed it, the process renames its own `.bak` back to `audiosync.exe` before it ends and records the version as blocked, so the next start runs the old version instead of finding no exe. Each of these writes S309's debug line with the step and the error code. Source: Microsoft Learn, MS-FSCC Zone.Identifier stream name; IAttachmentExecute::Save; Enable and configure Microsoft Defender Antivirus protection features; System Error Codes 0 to 499; AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-27).
- **S316** *Required.* *No retry storm.* The updater never repeats a download, an unpack or a swap on its own: each takes a click on Download and Install, or on Try Again in S302's other-copy case, and S315's lock retries are the only repeat without a click. A version that failed its first start (S314) or was blocked (S315) is stored in the settings file with that reason. The launch check treats the stored version as no update (no notice, one debug line naming the version and the reason) for as long as it is the newest candidate; a manual check still offers it, and its S297 notice adds that this version was blocked or didn't start last time. A higher version found later clears the record and is offered as usual, and so does any completed install. Other failures (offline, a bad signature, a broken archive, disk full, a read-only folder, another copy running) store nothing, so the next due check (S305) offers the same version again. Source: AudioSync design (advisor decision 2026-10-27).
